Scientists uncover genetic clues to how Chornobyl’s dogs survived

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

When people fled the Chornobyl nuclear disaster in 1986, they had to leave their pets behind.

Some dogs escaped the crews sent to kill them, and the free-roaming dogs there today likely descend at least partly from those pets.

Today, dogs living at the power plant are genetically distinct from dogs in Chornobyl town, just 10 miles (16 km) away. A new study suggests natural selection may help explain that gap.

Many genes near the stretches of DNA that caught the researchers’ attention help repair DNA, fight infection, or play a role in cancer.

The findings offer clues about how the dogs survived, though they don’t prove that these genes helped.

Megan Dillon, a postdoctoral research scholar at North Carolina State University’s College of Veterinary Medicine, led the work with Matthew Breen, a professor of genomics there.

A genetic divide

“While we can’t say for certain whether what we’ve found can be directly confirmed as selection, we do find these genomic signatures suggestive of selection,” Dillon said.

“When we dig deeper into those genomic regions, we find that these genes are involved in biologically relevant pathways that could allow them to overcome the effects of being exposed to toxicants in this environment.”

Earlier studies found a surprising divide between the two groups. Town dogs were genetically closer to dogs in Poland and Russia than to the plant dogs, even though free-breeding dogs across Eastern Europe live much farther apart.

Earlier research had examined breed ancestry, physical separation, and inbreeding without fully explaining the genetic divide.

A separate study also found no evidence that radiation had accelerated mutations in the plant dogs.

That left two main possibilities: genetic drift, where genes become more common by chance because some dogs happen to have more puppies, and natural selection.

With selection, dogs carrying genes that help them survive and reproduce pass those genes on more often.

Comparing the dogs’ DNA

Veterinarians collected samples in 2018 and 2019 while treating dogs at spay and vaccine clinics run by the Clean Futures Fund.

The team began with complete genomes, or full sets of DNA, from 106 dogs, now available in a public database.

Researchers then removed close relatives and dogs whose DNA looked more like the other group’s. That left 33 plant dogs and 30 town dogs.

Genetic drift has a stronger effect in smaller breeding populations, so the researchers estimated how many dogs contributed genes to each group.

The plant dogs didn’t consistently have a smaller breeding population, weakening the idea that a small group alone explained the gap.

Patterns in the DNA also helped the researchers trace the dogs’ population history.

The plant population appeared to start shrinking about 35 to 40 generations ago, around the time of the 1986 disaster and the culling that followed.

The authors think most of today’s plant dogs descend from pets in Pripyat, the workers’ city about two miles (three km) from the plant. Residents left the city 36 hours after the explosion.

An island comparison

The team turned to dogs from the Galápagos Islands to explore what isolation could explain. Those dogs had lived apart from other populations and faced an attempted cull, but they had never lived beside a ruined reactor.

This comparison included 250 dogs from eight groups, including village dogs from Poland and Russia.

On a standard measure of genetic difference, the plant dogs were about twice as far from each of three Galápagos groups as the town dogs were.

The island dogs were genetically closer to the town dogs and Europe’s village dogs than to the plant dogs. Isolation on islands hadn’t produced the same degree of difference.

A computer program also sorted all 250 dogs into groups based only on their DNA. It identified three clusters, including one made up entirely of power plant dogs.

Differences across chromosomes

The researchers then compared individual chromosomes, the packages that hold DNA. They expected random drift to affect the chromosomes broadly alike, but some stood out much more than others.

In the full-genome data, comparisons involving plant dogs varied about four times as much between chromosomes as comparisons involving the other groups.

Chromosomes 8, 14, 26, and 27 consistently showed the greatest differences.

On chromosome 8, the genetic difference between plant dogs and a purebred group was nearly 50% greater than their difference across the whole genome.

The plant dogs also retained their overall genetic variety, unlike the loss often seen when drift affects a small population.

Narrowing the search

Next, the team examined about 4.8 million places where dogs’ DNA commonly varies.

They compared plant dogs with town dogs, mixed-breed dogs, and 24 purebreds from eight breeds, including Chihuahuas and golden retrievers.

The plant dogs stood out at 1,138 spots, most of them packed into clusters. That pattern can develop when selection favors a particular stretch of DNA.

The researchers ran four more tests on each region to look for other signs of selection. Every region passed at least two tests, and 111 passed at least three.

What the genes do

The team identified 247 genes in or near those regions. Cancer-related pathways showed the strongest associations, including those linked to melanoma and cancers of the urinary and reproductive systems.

Other leading functions involved copying and repairing DNA, controlling when cells die, and managing immune and inflammatory responses.

These links make the genes worth studying, but they don’t show that the dogs resist cancer or other damage.

Other species in the exclusion zone have offered similar clues: birds showed signs of adapting to oxidative stress, a form of cell damage, while silver birch and evening primrose showed improved DNA repair.

In gray wolves, genes involved in DNA damage and immunity were active at different levels.

Did the dogs adapt?

The researchers still don’t know how the genetic differences arose.

“When we consider the toxic environment around Chornobyl, I can see why most people would think, ‘Surely there’s got to have been some consequence of living in that environment,'” Breen said.

“But what we’re saying is the consequence may not have gone as far as changing the dogs’ genome in order to adapt, it could be the ones that were able to survive the initial exposures had this level of underlying genetic diversity and variants to offer a higher degree of fitness,” Breen added.

“Obviously that’s speculation, and we don’t know exactly what’s happened.”

The study compared DNA without measuring the dogs’ health or their individual exposures. The team also lacks DNA from their ancestors before 1986, so it can’t connect any particular gene to a specific hazard.

Following the clues

The authors see dogs as useful stand-ins for studying the exposures people face.

As nuclear power expands, the researchers say understanding how long-term environmental exposures affect animals could help scientists assess risks following future accidents.

Future research will need to determine whether these genetic differences actually helped the dogs survive.

Until then, Chornobyl’s dogs offer an intriguing glimpse of how animal populations may respond to extreme environmental pressures.

The study is published in the journal Genes.

Drought and wildfire are quietly weakening Earth’s carbon defenses

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

When drought and wildfire hit an ecosystem at the same time, something quietly breaks in the relationship between plants and water. 

A global satellite study has found that vegetation hit by these compound events becomes markedly more sensitive to rainfall. Yet, it is simultaneously less efficient at turning that water into carbon gain. 

The two processes, which normally move together, start to come apart. This finding’s implications for the health of the world’s forests are not reassuring.

The study was conducted by Huipeng Xi and Qunming Wang at Tongji University in Shanghai.

The carbon sink under pressure

Every year, vegetation absorbs enormous quantities of carbon dioxide through photosynthesis.

Much of that carbon eventually returns to the atmosphere, but the portion that remains stored in plants and soils helps slow climate change.

This service is not guaranteed, though. It depends on ecosystems remaining functional, which in turn depends heavily on water. 

Precipitation is the primary driver of plant growth across much of the globe. How sensitively vegetation photosynthesis responds to rainfall variability is a key indicator of ecosystem health.

After drought and wildfire, however, a sharp increase in that sensitivity may signal that vegetation is struggling to recover.

Nearly 200,000 compound events

The researchers analyzed global satellite data from 2001 to 2022. They used three vegetation proxies to track photosynthetic activity at fine spatial resolution. 

These measurements included faint light emitted by plants during photosynthesis and two satellite-based indicators of vegetation greenness and growth.

Drought was identified using two complementary indices. One was based on precipitation deficits (meteorological drought). The other used terrestrial water storage anomalies derived from satellite gravimetry (hydrological drought). 

Fire was tracked using ESA’s burned-area dataset.

The team identified 72,088 compound meteorological drought–fire events across the globe between 2001 and 2022, and 108,373 compound hydrological drought–fire events. 

A compound event was defined as drought and fire striking the same location in the same month. To keep the signal clean, no other major disturbance could have occurred within a year before or after.

More rain sensitivity, less efficiency

Across the full study period, global vegetation sensitivity to precipitation declined. But in the one to three years after a compound drought–fire event, that sensitivity jumped back up more sharply than after drought alone. 

Hydrological drought–fire events drove the strongest response: sensitivity increased by 7.89×10⁻⁵ mm⁻¹ using the fluorescence measure, compared to 3.20×10⁻⁵ mm⁻¹ for meteorological drought–fire combinations. 

Around 54.6% of all vegetated areas showed increased sensitivity in the aftermath of compound events.

At the same time, water-use efficiency – the ratio of carbon gained to water consumed – declined across most of those same areas. 

The two processes, which in a healthy ecosystem track each other reasonably closely, were pulling apart.

“Our findings reveal a critical vulnerability: after compound drought–fire events, ecosystems become more dependent on rainfall but less efficient at converting water into carbon gain,” Wang said. 

“This decoupling suggests that vegetation may be entering a fragile recovery state, where increased rain sensitivity does not translate into healthier carbon–water balance – a warning signal for ecosystem resilience under escalating climate extremes.”

What’s driving the shift

To understand why some areas showed stronger responses than others, the team applied a random forest model to 22 environmental variables. It explained 79% of the spatial variation in sensitivity changes. 

The top predictors were atmospheric aridity and soil nutrients, particularly nitrogen and phosphorus. Where the air was drier and soils more nutrient-depleted, post-disturbance sensitivity rose more steeply.

The finding points to a compounding feedback. Drought dries the air and depletes soil moisture. Fire consumes organic matter and disrupts nutrient cycling. 

Recovering vegetation may therefore face drier atmospheric conditions and disrupted nutrient supplies.

Together, these pressures could help explain why rainfall becomes more influential even as water-use efficiency declines.

A warning for the carbon cycle

Compound drought–fire events are not rare anomalies. The study catalogued nearly 200,000 of them across two decades. 

Climate projections suggest they will become more frequent as temperatures rise.

If each one leaves the affected ecosystem more dependent on precipitation while less capable of using it efficiently, the cumulative effect on the terrestrial carbon sink could be substantial.

Vegetation that has decoupled its carbon and water processes is also harder to predict. 

Earth system models that assume a stable relationship between rainfall and photosynthesis may misrepresent these ecosystems.

They could potentially overestimate the carbon sink in regions with high compound disturbance frequency. 

The authors suggest that vapor pressure deficit and soil nutrient status could serve as early-warning indicators.

The satellite record reveals a troubling pattern: when drought and wildfire strike together, their effects on vegetation can persist for years after the immediate disturbance ends.

Even when plants begin to recover, they may become more dependent on rainfall while growing less efficient at using water to absorb carbon.

As climate extremes intensify, that imbalance could weaken the ability of forests and other ecosystems to help regulate Earth’s climate.

The study is published in the Journal of Remote Sensing.

Children can be afraid of wild animals and still love nature, scientists find

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A girl in Congo told researchers she was afraid of seven of the 10 animals they asked her about. Yet she still liked visiting the forest.

“I like to visit the forest. There are trees to protect me,” she said. She wasn’t alone in feeling both ways about nature.

Among 295 children in the United States and the Democratic Republic of Congo, fear of animals had little connection to their interest in nature.

Children who feared wildlife were often just as attracted to forests, gardens, and other natural settings as those who weren’t afraid.

Fear leaves room for curiosity

Vanessa Woods, a research scientist in Duke University’s Department of Evolutionary Anthropology, thought the most fearful children would show the least interest in nature.

“Instead, we found this fear could coexist with an attraction to the natural world in general,” Woods said.

The children were visiting a zoo or a bonobo sanctuary, where they answered questions and chose between photographs.

Those choices tell us what they said they liked, though they can’t tell us what the children would actually do in a forest.

In both countries, fear of animals had little connection to which nature scenes children preferred.

Even children afraid of beetles were no less likely to choose a backyard scene containing beetles and butterflies.

The team suggested that fear of a particular animal and interest in nature may do different jobs: one responds to possible danger, while the other draws a child toward places full of life.

A matter of temperament

In his 1984 book Biophilia, biologist E. O. Wilson argued that people are born with an emotional attraction to other living things. He saw it as an instinct everyone shares.

That idea helped inspire decades of research on nature’s psychological benefits. But the authors say evidence that everyone shares this instinct remains limited.

In 2023, Woods and Melinda Knuth of North Carolina State University proposed that attraction to nature might be a temperament trait, much like being outgoing or eager to try new things.

Some people would feel a strong pull toward nature, others a mild interest, and some little interest or even fear.

Drawing on earlier child-development research, the team used four signs of a temperament trait: it varies between people, appears early, stays fairly steady, and ideally has measurable signs in the body or brain.

This study looked for the first two.

Pictures and preferences

The children were between three and 13 years old. In North Carolina, 124 took part while visiting the North Carolina Zoo with their families; in Congo, 171 came on school trips to Lola ya Bonobo, a sanctuary for orphaned bonobos near Kinshasa.

The sanctuary paid for tickets and transport, so the visit didn’t depend on a family being able to afford it.

Sessions there ran in French, except for 64 children from two schools who spoke only Lingala and worked with a bilingual educator trained by the researcher.

Each child rated their fear of 10 animals from a standard children’s fear survey, including snakes, wolves, ants, and cats. They could say they were not afraid, afraid, or very afraid.

The photo choices included a pollinator garden or a plain lawn, and a rainforest or Venice. For a backyard, children chose between butterflies alone and a mix of butterflies and beetles.

Differences begin early

Some children consistently preferred wilder settings, while others favored cities, lawns, and more carefully maintained landscapes.

Even the youngest children, most of whom were American, differed in what appealed to them.

Woods had seen something similar in her own family. Her son loved mud and insects, while her daughter preferred gardens and neatly kept landscapes.

“You know why I keep [choosing] forest, forest, forest? I love a forest!” a five-year-old boy said.

A four-year-old chose cities instead, because “Mom can drive me around.”

The appeal of cities

American children feared spiders, ants, and beetles more, while Congolese children feared bears, wolves, snakes, and lizards more. Their overall fear scores ended up fairly close, with Congolese children slightly higher.

American children were more willing to welcome wildlife near home. They also chose the wilder travel destination in 46% of their choices, compared with 28% among Congolese children.

All the Congolese children lived in Kinshasa, a fast-growing city where tree cover is shrinking and wildlife rarely features in daily life. Many praised cities for being safe, clean, and pretty.

“The city is very pretty… the other place might have a crocodile,” an 11-year-old girl said.

A place to play

Garden choices didn’t differ between the countries. Some Kinshasa children liked lawns because they offered somewhere to play.

“I love the lawn, I would play with my dolls,” another 11-year-old girl said.

In Kinshasa, lawns and tidy gardens often need land, water, and household staff, resources mainly available to wealthier families.

The authors suggested that a child’s choice might reflect the life they hope for as well as their feelings about nature.

Overall, children in both countries chose the wilder, more varied pictures about half the time or less.

The researchers found that surprising, since visiting a zoo or sanctuary might encourage children to choose nature and push scores higher than they would be elsewhere.

More to learn

The findings don’t yet establish attraction to nature as a temperament trait.

The study did not establish whether these preferences persist as children grow or have measurable signs in the body or brain.

The groups differed in more than nationality. American children visited the zoo with families who could afford tickets, potentially introducing differences in their exposure to animals and interest in nature.

Children living near or within Congo Basin forests might also answer differently from city children, and the authors want future research to include them.

There is a lesson for conservation campaigns, too. If only some people feel drawn to nature, messages that assume everyone already cares about biodiversity may fail to reach many people.

Woods is discussing possible research with Sarah P. Duke Gardens, where children could explore a varied native plant garden and the carefully planned Discovery Garden.

That would let researchers see whether the photo choices match what children do outdoors.

“I think it’d be really interesting just to see where kids go and what they’re attracted to, and how they interact with different things that they’re afraid of or nervous about,” she said.

“Don’t give up on your kid because they are terrified of one forest creature,” Woods said. “There is still room for wonder.”

The study is published in the journal Frontiers in Psychology.

STAT+: What are the health stakes of the midterms? Policymakers, lobbyists, and experts weigh in

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

WASHINGTON — No matter what happens on Election Day, the midterms will have sweeping implications for American health care.

The future of essential health policies, the politics surrounding health issues, and insight into the inner workings of the remade federal health infrastructure will all be affected by Tuesday’s outcomes. A Congress controlled by Democrats could slow down policymaking and amp up investigations. If Republicans maintain a majority, on the other hand, that could supercharge the party’s legislative agenda.   

Over the last several weeks, STAT interviewed more than two dozen government and private sector leaders, consultants, lobbyists, and staffers. Below are some of the top ways Tuesday’s vote could impact the U.S. health system.

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Votebeat is a nonprofit news organization reporting on voting access and election administration across the U.S. Sign up for Votebeat Texas’ free newsletter here.

This story was produced with support from the Pulitzer Center.

ALAMO, Tex. – Eustorgia Santos de Torres has lived here for four decades. Last year, she turned to a trusted Rio Grande Valley nonprofit for help navigating the citizenship application process. The same nonprofit — La Unión del Pueblo Entero, or LUPE — then helped her register to vote, and she cast her first ballot as a citizen in the primary election last March.

Santos de Torres, 67, is retired after years cleaning houses and working as a healthcare aide. She speaks mainly Spanish and is the primary caretaker for her husband, Nemorio, who is blind. “Yo soy sus ojos,” Santos de Torres said — I am his eyes.

But last month, Santos de Torres, a new voter, struggled to help Nemorio, an American-born citizen, understand and cast his mail ballot for the November election. All the information on the ballot is in English, she said, and the letters are small.

So Santos de Torres helped Nemorio to the car and made the familiar 7-minute drive to LUPE’s headquarters in the neighboring town of San Juan to ask for help with her husband’s ballot.

Related

How Trump is prepping to deny election results

This time, though, LUPE workers reluctantly turned the couple away.

In 2021, Texas’ GOP-dominated state Legislature passed a sweeping and controversial overhaul of election laws known as SB1. Among other changes, SB1 made it illegal for anyone to receive compensation for helping voters fill in their ballots.

That not only makes it illegal for paid nonprofit staff to help voters, but also criminalized common past practices, such as organizations giving volunteers gas money or stipends. Violations are a felony punishable by up to 10 years in prison and a fine of up to $10,000.

In the wake of the law, Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton, a Republican currently running in the state’s nationally watched U.S. Senate race, investigated and prosecuted voting rights advocacy groups and organizers in heavily Latino communities across the state.

Groups such as LUPE say they have been forced to alter or stop some voter outreach as they worry about running afoul of the law. LUPE used to send workers out walking blocks to help voters. Now it has to instead ask volunteers to do more limited versions of that work.

Latinos and other non-English speaking voters will be key to the outcome of the state’s high-profile U.S. Senate and House races this year. But the law means such voters, as well as those who are disabled and elderly, have fewer options for help.

LUPE is known for providing one-on-one assistance to people in the Latino community, so the restrictions on helping with voting are a real pain point for the organization, Executive Director Tania Chavez Camacho said. Texas has strict rules limiting who is eligible to use mail ballots, but people over 65 may do so. LUPE helps around 30,000 people a year, she said, including people with disabilities and many older people who, each year, “want to vote by mail and they come in wanting help.”

Start your day with essential news from Salon.
Sign up for our free morning newsletter, Crash Course.

Now, Chavez Camacho said, when the group holds public town halls for residents to instruct them on how to vote, if a voter pulls out their mail ballot to ask for help, organizers must ask the voter to leave so paid employees aren’t near the ballot.

And if workers for LUPE’s advocacy arm are canvassing for candidates and a voter shows them a mail ballot and asks for help filling it out, workers have been trained to say they can’t and walk away, Chavez Camacho said.

“We literally have to turn them away,” Chavez Camacho said. “We’ve had to do a lot of training with our staff for the past three years because they were so accustomed to helping folks one-on-one and now they can’t.”

Santos de Torres did what she could to help her husband, who mailed back his ballot about a month ago.

“But I don’t think it’s right that LUPE can’t help us,” she told a reporter in Spanish. “That law is not right. It’s excluding people who need help.”

“What if,” she added, “my husband didn’t have me?”

‘A person who doesn’t know how to read has to do it on their own’

The Rio Grande Valley, where LUPE is based, covers roughly 4,300-square-miles across Hidalgo, Cameron, Starr, and Willacy counties along the Mexico border at Texas’ southern tip. It’s a diverse community with some residents who primarily speak Spanish. Younger generations of residents easily switch between English and Spanish in conversation.

Many households are made up of families whose members have mixed legal statuses, including some who have lived in the area for generations. The Valley also has a history of activism as the birthplace of the Texas farmworker movement of the 1960’s. Farmworkers – who harvested cotton, vegetables and fruits — organized and marched for roughly 400 miles from the Valley to Austin to demand fair wages and better working conditions. Mexican-American farmworker labor today remains key to the area’s growth and agricultural economy.

But due to the lack of affordable housing, Mexican Americans for years purchased land they could afford in rural areas outside of city limits, often lacking basic infrastructure and public services. Those communities are known now as the colonias.

Alamo, where Santos de Torres lives, and San Juan, where LUPE is based, are neighboring cities. The landscape varies depending on the side of town. In the southern parts of the cities, closer to the border with Mexico, modest residential homes with green front lawns adorn along palm tree-lined streets. Shopping centers, chain restaurants, stadiums, and commercial developments lie to the north of the cities, along Interstate Highway 2, reflecting the transition from the once primarily agricultural community to a growing metropolitan hub. The Valley has for years relied on its trade of goods and services with Mexico. It has a population of more than 1.4 million people and 90% of them are Hispanic or Latino, according to Census data.

About 24% of people in the Valley live below the poverty line, which is more than double the national rate. And about 30% of Valley residents don’t have health insurance – one of the highest uninsured rates in the country. The area also has one of the highest rates of diabetes in the country; Nemorio Torres’ blindness is a complication of the disease.

LUPE and other advocacy groups have long worked with residents in the colonias and all over the Valley, providing resources to find affordable housing and medical treatment, or helping with complex procedures such as filing taxes. Voter education and outreach was part of the mission.

Now, instead of providing one-on-one assistance, LUPE hosts group sessions with a screen projector showing what mail ballot applications and mail ballots look like. Facilitators point at the screen indicating what the instructions say and explaining how the forms should be filled out.

The limitations are frustrating, Chavez Camacho said, especially “si una persona no sabe leer” — if a person can’t read. “It doesn’t matter that we’re doing those sessions because a person who doesn’t know how to read has to do it on their own and we can’t help them to know where to mark,” she said.

Instead, LUPE’s workers and volunteers now focus on letting people know key deadlines are coming up. They also have fewer volunteers willing to guide and interpret for voters at the polls, she said, because the law now requires such people to sign an oath affirming the voter is qualified to receive help because they are disabled or need an interpreter, and detail their relationship with the voter. They must also affirm they are not being paid by a candidate or political committee, which would be illegal. Volunteers fear any misstep while assisting voters could get them in trouble, Chavez-Camacho said.

“We really feel that the people are not getting the support they need,” Chavez Camacho said.

Election and voting law experts say the courts have interpreted “compensation” prohibited from going to volunteers by SB1 as any type of benefit. That interpretation is so broad that it makes it difficult for voter outreach organizations to know whether they are violating the law, said Zachary Dolling, litigation director at Texas Civil Rights Project, which works with groups to design trainings in voter outreach that comply with the law.

Dolling said some organizations historically provided unpaid volunteers dinner, T-shirts, or pens, and others have given volunteers college credit for their work.

“All of those things are items of value” and could be considered compensation, he said.

Advocates warned state leaders of added burdens for people with disabilities

Texas Republicans passed SB1, the sweeping overhaul of election law, in part responding to false claims and conspiracy theories about the 2020 election.

Republicans claimed the law would increase public trust and confidence in the elections. Democrats argued the legislation would disproportionately burden voters of color and people with disabilities, and staged two walkouts and a 15-hour Senate filibuster in their failed attempt to defeat the bill.

Despite lawsuits from voting rights organizations including LUPE, the League of United Latin American Citizens and organizations representing Texans with disabilities, a federal appeals court ultimately upheld key provisions of SB1, including the restrictions on voter assistance.

Other attempts to expand voting help for people with disabilities were meanwhile unsuccessful.

In 2023, Gov. Greg Abbott, a Republican, vetoed a bipartisan bill that would have expanded vote-by-mail access for people with disabilities — specifically people who are blind or paralyzed and need assistance marking their ballot — by allowing such voters to request an electronic ballot and use a computer to mark their choices. Similar options in Texas are already available for military overseas voters and astronauts, but not for voters with disabilities.

The challenges Santos de Torres and her husband have faced are an example of what advocates worried about under SB1, said Lydia Nunez Landry, statewide coordinator for REV UP Texas, which works to get out the vote among people with disabilities.

“That was our worst fear and now it’s happening,” she said. Nunez Landry has muscular dystrophy, uses a power wheelchair, and needs help doing basic tasks such as opening the refrigerator.

The state has made voting “just so impossible and so arduous for people — and now people are also terrified of helping us,” she said.

An increased ‘sense of anxiety’

Groups across the state have been changing their voter outreach work to comply with SB1.

Asian Texans for Justice conducts voter outreach in Asian communities, focusing on San Antonio, Houston, Austin, and North Texas.

The stricter rules associated with voting by mail under SB1 have created “a sense of anxiety” in those communities, Executive Director Nabila Mansoor said. She said that the process is now much more complicated for elderly voters, who must provide one of two forms of ID on their application and pay close attention to pages of instructions that are written in font that is small and hard for them to read. In addition, an error could force them to restart the whole process of applying for a ballot.

“That anxiety comes from this kind of fear that they’re going to do something wrong,” Mansoor said. “Whenever you have that kind of fear, it is one more barrier for that person to actually go out and vote.”

Now that Asian Texans for Justice can no longer provide one-on-one help, they’ve turned to providing checklists for voters, she said, which she believes is less effective when some voters may need help reading and understanding the ballot and instructions

Mansoor said she believes that SB1’s goal was to make it harder for people to vote.

“It means that we have to be more careful. It means that we have to dot our i’s and cross our t’s. It means that we have to take good records. It means that we have to be very thorough in terms of our training of our volunteers,” she said, adding that the organization assembled a legal team to defend against challenges to their work as well.

In the meantime, volunteers for LUPE and other groups say they are doing their best to fill the gap.

Maria Gomez, better known as Doña Mary, of Pharr, worked as a farmworker for years in Hidalgo County and has been a member of LUPE since the organization was founded. Gomez, 78, was involved in the farmworker movement years ago. She now ensures her elderly friends and neighbors are aware of election deadlines, know where to vote, and have a ride to the polls if they need one.

After she drops off her grandkids and great-grandkids at school, she makes her rounds around the Colonias in San Juan in her small red SUV. She chats up her neighbors, and asks them what they need help with or if they need resources or a ride. She’s always fielding calls.

“People don’t vote because sometimes they can’t leave their home and because they might not have access to that piece of paper that they need to sign to receive their ballot,” Gomez said in Spanish, referring to voter registration forms and ballot-by-mail applications.

One of her friends, 80-year-old Teresa Corona Garza of San Juan, has relied on Gomez for rides to the polls for some time now. Corona Garza struggles to stand for long periods of time and can’t see very well out of her left eye. Even though she qualifies to vote by mail, she prefers to go to the polls in person.

“There’s so much on there you have to fill out, and those letters are small,” Corona Garza said in Spanish. “No, I prefer to go, quiero ver que mi voto cuenta,” she said – I want to see that my vote is counted.

The laws have gotten so complicated and strict that Gomez worries state leaders may even try to prevent volunteers like her from providing rides to the polls.

“I don’t know what’s going to happen, if they’re going to keep making this harder for people,” she said, “but I would like to see people who want to vote to be able to do it.”

Natalia Contreras covers election administration and voting access for Votebeat in partnership with the Texas Tribune. Natalia is based in Corpus Christi. Contact her at ncontreras@votebeat.org.

5 things ancients knew about finding happiness

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Below, June Cohen shares five key insights from her new book, The Great Remembering: Powerful Ancient Practices―And the New Science Bringing Them Back.

Cohen is a pioneer in media and technology, best known as the co-creator of TED Talks, Masters of Scale, and Meditative Story. During her 11 years at TED, she cohosted the flagship event—working with hundreds of speakers on their talks—and launched TED Talks online. She’s earned more than 100 industry awards, including 67 Webby honors, 8 Apple “Podcast of the Year” awards, and a Peabody.

What’s the Big Idea?

Ancient cultures worldwide developed wisdom systems: embodied practices passed down by example that helped people navigate hardship, build connection, and find happiness. Modern science is now proving they work.

Listen to the audio version of this Book Bite—read by Cohen herself—in the Next Big Idea app, or buy the book.

1. An epic physical challenge can help you through an epic emotional one.

The first wisdom system that fascinated me was the epic physical challenge: a practice of pushing the body to its limits. To the Western mind, it doesn’t always make logical sense. But it was everywhere in the ancient world. In boys’ coming-of-age rituals, the boy might go on a hunt or swim an icy river. That physical challenge marked the emotional transition from boy to man. Or think of pilgrimage—a challenging journey—often taken during a difficult transition, like when you need to release grief or seek forgiveness. There is this ancient pattern of physical challenges pairing with emotional ones.

We still do this today. You get divorced, so you run a marathon. You recover from cancer, so you climb a mountain. We don’t know why, but we reach for something hard and physical when we face something hard and emotional.

New science tells us our instincts are exactly right. It’s the science of “hormetic stress,” or good stress. This isn’t emotional stress—it’s the physical stress of something genuinely dangerous: extreme exertion, fasting, intense heat or cold. When your body encounters these challenges, it triggers a chain reaction of renewal that goes down to the cellular level. One of these processes is called autophagy—sometimes described as “spring cleaning” for cells. The scientist who discovered it won the 2016 Nobel Prize in Medicine.

But the most interesting thing about an epic physical challenge is what happens in the brain. If you have read Michael Pollan’s book on psychedelics, How to Change Your Mind, you know the most sought-after effect of psychedelics is neuroplasticity—the brain’s ability to create new connections. An epic physical challenge also creates neuroplasticity. Mark Mattson of Johns Hopkins told me, “We and others have evidence that your nerve cells actually grow and form new synapses in response to physical exercise”—and to a lesser extent, intermittent fasting. This isn’t just the motor cortex; it includes regions like the hippocampus, which governs learning and memory. The neuroplasticity from an epic physical challenge might help rewrite your story when you’re going through a big emotional challenge.

That might be why so many people you know run marathons, go on pilgrimage, or sign up for weeklong fasting, silent meditation, or darkness retreats. The ancients knew that taking on an epic physical challenge when you’re faced with an epic emotional challenge is one way to make yourself happy.

2. There’s a right way (and a wrong way) to take on epic physical challenges.

Think of this as a public service announcement from your ancestors: Across cultures and across time, the same elements determine whether an epic physical challenge is performed successfully.

  • Structure. An epic physical challenge is dangerous. It can kill you if you’re not careful. You need guardrails that push you without harming you.
  • Meaning. Who will you be on the other side? What transition will it see you through? Symbolism is a huge part of why it works.
  • A coach. Someone who knows the ropes and believes in you.
  • A witnessing community. People who can say, “You’ve done it. You’ve served us.” We humans have a deep need to be witnessed and to serve.
  • Nature. Thousands of studies show that just 20 minutes in a green space can boost you biologically.
  • Recovery. Cells repair, and brain cells grow during recovery, not during the challenge itself. If you skip the recovery, you skip the growth.
  • The squad. The people you train with, strain with, and cross the finish line with.

These different elements combine to create something holistic and human. Each of us needs slightly different things at different times to help us through tough transitions. These elements transform an epic physical challenge from something you endured into a wisdom system—something that works on you at every level.

3. Your body is a powerful pathway to your mind and your mood.

Ancient festivals were extraordinarily physical: People traveled great distances, climbed mountains, fasted, stayed up all night, danced for hours. The message was clearly that spiritual transformation begins with the body.

If that sounds too mystical, here’s the science: The single clearest explanation, in one word, is endorphins. The ancients didn’t use that word, but they had an exquisite understanding of how endorphins work and how essential they are to our happiness.

You’ve probably heard of endorphins as our natural pain reliever—the body’s own opioid. You may know them as the source of the “runner’s high,” that flood of good feeling that long-distance runners experience mid-run. (I ran cross-country in high school and never felt anything like a high during a run, but I did notice the post-run glow, that sense of Oh, I can handle life. It turns out the “post-run high” is actually more common.) That’s what endorphins feel like: a quiet confidence, a lift. Robin Dunbar at Oxford told me they’re the most effective antidepressant you can get, and hundreds of studies back him up.

But exercise isn’t the only way to access endorphins. We get them any time we encounter physical pain—including extreme heat or cold, as in a sauna or cold plunge. We get them during sex. And, most fascinatingly, we get them through synchrony with other people.

Modern Western culture tends to treat the brain and body as separate, with the brain firmly in charge. You might trace that assumption to René Descartes, the 17th-century philosopher who declared mind and body to be distinct substances. One scientist I interviewed put it bluntly: “He has a lot to answer for, because there is no mind-body distinction.” The ancients knew this. Science is finally catching up.

4. Don’t go it alone.

Let me start here with a story. It’s a story about ultramarathoner Lisa Hallett. She’s setting the pace for all six in her group as they descend in darkness toward the Carbon River. Lisa can smell the river long before she can see or hear it. It’s the 18th hour of a continuous 150K run around Mount Rainier in Washington state. She and her team will climb and descend a total of 27,000 feet before they sleep again.

Lisa is hungry—her food ran out hours ago—and hunger makes it hard to regulate your temperature. She told me, “Am I cold? Am I hot? It’s too much. And then you start spiraling. I can’t do this. This is too hard.”

Then, three miles from the next aid station, she hears her 15-year-old daughter giggling. Her daughter Heidi and a friend have run in to meet her, carrying peanut butter and jelly and snacks, fresh and positive. Lisa feels a rush of gratitude and a surge of new energy. She picks up the pace as her group runs in unison to the campground.

This is not just emotional. It is physical and measurable. Along with the runner’s high, there is something Robin Dunbar’s lab at Oxford identified as the rower’s high: When people row together rather than alone, they produce twice as many endorphins.

And here’s the best part: You don’t have to row or run to get it. We produce endorphins any time we synchronize with other people—singing in harmony, line dancing, laughing together, or even eating a shared feast (apparently you have to eat until you’re painfully full for it to trigger the effect). This is why motorcyclists ride in packs, why yoga feels easier in a class than in your living room, and why group singing has been shown to function as an antidepressant.

5. Modern science is validating what you already know in your bones.

When I first began this investigation, I expected tension between ancient wisdom and modern science. What I found instead was an emerging partnership. Many scientists are using modern tools to validate the traditional systems they grew up with, and intuitively know to work.

Scientists in Finland proved the profound health benefits of the sauna and cold plunge. Muslim scientists have been proving the health benefits of intermittent fasting, as practiced during Ramadan. Scientists in Japan have shown the physiological health benefits of forest bathing.

I didn’t grow up with the sauna or Ramadan. I learned about those benefits from studies. But I did grow up in a house surrounded by trees that my father planted himself. When I read forest bathing studies, I thought: I know that works.

That’s been the response I’ve heard most often since starting to share this work. After one talk, a couple came up to me. The woman said she had elbowed her husband during my section on group dancing: “See—we should dance more.” Her husband had elbowed her right back during the nature section: “See—we should hike more.” They both already knew what made them happy. The science just gave them permission to act on it.

And the beauty of all of this is: You probably kind of already know this stuff. Because while the science and history might be new to you, you probably already know in your bones that these things make you happy. When we look through this dual lens—on one hand, this is what people have always done, and on the other, this is what science tells us—it gives us a powerful mirror to hold up to our own lives, and remember.

This article originally appeared in Next Big Idea Club magazine and is reprinted with permission.

The Healthcare Election: Florida

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

When federal subsidies for Affordable Care Act health insurance expired last year, Florida was hit particularly hard. Now, as the election and open enrollment coincide, healthcare is a major issue.

Campbell Hatton on that night at the AO Arena, his dad Ricky, 30 yard screamers and mental health

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
'It's easy for people to sort of shy away from things that bring up those emotions'
📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The term cardio often elicits grumbles as people picture punishing treadmill sessions and trail runs. But add the word cozy before it, and cardio is suddenly…a lot more palatable. That’s the gist behind the “cozy cardio” trend, which took off on social media in 2024 and resurfaced in 2026. The concept is simple: perform low- to moderate-intensity workouts in a comfortable, inviting environment.

Cozy cardio could look like setting out a few candles in your living room, dimming the lights, and turning on your favorite Netflix show while you stroll on a walking pad. Or it could involve donning a comfy velvet sweatsuit and sipping a delicious beverage while going for an easy walk on your favorite trail or through your neighborhood.

Cozy cardio looks different for everyone, according to Hope Zuckerbrow, the TikToker credited with starting the trend. But no matter how you practice it, the overarching objective is the same: take the pressure off exercise and actually enjoy movement.

As a certified personal trainer, I wholeheartedly support that notion and think there are several key benefits that outdoor athletes could reap from embracing the trend.

Cozy Cardio Can Keep You Consistent When Life Gets in the Way–or Motivation Wanes

When it comes to exercise, many people have an all-or-nothing mindset—especially athletes who take their outdoor sport seriously. They have a set workout plan—for example, a five-mile run or an hour-long strength session—and when something thwarts it, they might throw in the towel and do nothing at all.

But staying consistent with your workout routine is key to reaping the mental and physical benefits of exercise. So a better approach is to still do some form of movement even if it looks different than what you’d originally envisioned. That’s where cozy cardio can come in.

Because the trend is all about lowering the stakes on exercise, it can feel really doable, even on those days when life gets in the way, or you’re not in the mood to do anything.

Plus, cozy cardio creates an environment that makes working out a pleasurable experience. And that’s important, because actually enjoying exercise is associated with actually sticking with a workout routine, according to research.

Gentle Movement Can Promote Recovery

Another benefit of cozy cardio is that it can serve as active recovery in the wake of long, hard workouts. Research suggests doing even six to ten minutes of active recovery after a workout may boost overall performance.

So engaging in a short cozy cardio session after a big hike, ride, or run could inject a little more energy into your next outdoor adventure.

Steady-State Cardio Can Bolster Your Aerobic Fitness

A 2026 study found that low-intensity exercises improve both aerobic fitness and cardiometabolic health, which refers to factors involving the heart and metabolism, including blood pressure and cholesterol.

If you choose a steady-state form of exercise for your cozy cardio session, such as walking or a bike ride, and keep your effort low, you’ll likely tick the box for Zone 1 or Zone 2 cardio—both of which, when done consistently, can help build your aerobic fitness, or your VO2 max. VO2 max describes how efficiently your body takes in oxygen and transports it to the rest of the body so that your muscles can use it during physical activity. The higher your VO2, the better your aerobic fitness.

Overall, one of the biggest benefits of improving your aerobic health is going longer and having more energy during your favorite outdoor sport.

Scientists discover a bacterial defense so strange they thought it was broken

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Scientists have discovered a bizarre bacterial defense system that works almost completely backward from anything seen before. Unlike typical bacterial immune systems that keep a deadly toxin under control until a virus attacks, this unusual mechanism waits until an infection begins before producing the DNA that switches on its lethal weapon. The infected bacterium then destroys itself, preventing the virus from multiplying and spreading to neighboring cells.

Idaho gets green light to enforce abortion ban in health-risk cases

📅 Oct 31, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

(CN) — A federal appeals court on Friday cleared Idaho to enforce its abortion ban against a doctor who wants to treat women whose pregnancies threaten their health, not just their lives.

A divided Ninth Circuit panel put on hold a federal judge’s ruling barring Idaho from prosecuting the doctor in those cases. The 2-1 order keeps the ruling frozen while Idaho’s appeal proceeds.

Dr. Stacy Seyb, a maternal-fetal medicine specialist, sued Idaho officials in 2024 seeking to perform abortions when a pregnancy poses a non-negligible risk of serious and lasting harm to a woman’s health, even if it isn’t life-threatening.

After a bench trial, U.S. District Judge Lynn Winmill agreed in August. He found that “the historical record shows that our legal tradition has consistently shielded health-preserving abortions from prosecution since the early days of the common law.”

“A pregnant woman’s health is not a state resource to be allocated at the legislature’s whim,” Winmill wrote in an 81-page ruling.

He barred enforcement of Idaho’s Defense of Life Act and Fetal Heartbeat Act when a doctor determines in good faith that continuing a pregnancy could cause serious harm or lead the woman to take her own life. He later narrowed the injunction to apply only to Seyb while Idaho appealed.

Idaho Attorney General Raúl Labrador and Ada County Prosecuting Attorney Jan Bennetts asked the Ninth Circuit to freeze the injunction. During Sept. 22 arguments, Idaho attorney Mathew Hoffmann of Alliance Defending Freedom said it would “create an abortion-on-demand regime.”

U.S. Circuit Judge Consuelo Callahan, who joined Friday’s majority, pressed the state during arguments on whether Dobbs’ silence on medical exceptions was significant.

“Why should we not read this omission as implicitly allowing a woman has a constitutional right to an abortion for extreme medical emergencies?” she asked.

In Friday’s order, the majority said Idaho is likely to win under the U.S. Supreme Court’s 2022 decision in Dobbs v. Jackson Women’s Health Organization, which overturned Roe v. Wade.

“We think it unlikely that Dr. Seyb will be able to square the district court’s perspective on the history and tradition concerning abortions with the Supreme Court’s perspective in Dobbs,” the majority wrote.

The majority also said Idaho has a strong interest in limiting abortions to those allowed under state law, which “clearly outweighs” the harm to Seyb from complying with the law during the appeal.

On abortions sought because of suicide risk, the majority was blunt.

“There are other methods for preventing suicide, and Idaho’s ‘profound interest’ in preserving fetal life reasonably supports the use of those means over the termination of pregnancy,” they wrote.

Judge Kim Wardlaw joined the majority but wrote separately.

“As an intermediate court of appeals, we are bound by the decisions of the Supreme Court,” she said.

She acknowledged the court may eventually have to consider whether a state abortion ban is so restrictive that it fails even the limited constitutional review remaining after Dobbs, but said that issue was not before the court.

Judge Marsha Berzon dissented, writing that the majority misread Dobbs, which she said ended the right to elective abortion but did not address the separate right under Roe and Planned Parenthood v. Casey to abortions needed to protect a woman’s life or health.

“Unless and until the Supreme Court says otherwise, the degree to which that holding survives Dobbs is an open question,” Berzon said.

She also pointed to the stakes for patients. With the stay in place, she wrote, “some women with serious pregnancy complications will during the pendency of this appeal be required to continue their pregnancies at serious, permanent risk to their health.”

“I respectfully, but emphatically, dissent,” Berzon said.

The panel consolidated Idaho’s appeals with Seyb’s cross-appeal. The first brief is due Dec. 18, and the order says it will strongly disfavor extension requests. It does not schedule oral arguments.

Seyb’s attorneys and Labrador’s office didn’t immediately respond to requests for comment.

For a genuine fright this Halloween, you can turn your gaze to the southern border, where ravenous, flesh-eating maggots are festering and have taken to eating people's pets.

This week, the country's New World Screwworm (NWS) infestation spread to a third state, Arizona, which joins New Mexico and Texas in being plagued by the horrifying parasites. To date, 74 animal cases have been identified in the US since the flies re-invaded the country in June.

The flies lay hundreds of eggs in wounds and moist orifices of animals, and the resulting voracious maggots feast on their victims. TRAVERSE CITY, MICHIGAN — Michigan's Democratic coalition is fragmenting in real time days before a critical Senate election.

After it seemed like former primary rivals Abdul El-Sayed and Rep. Haley Stevens might finally appear together, they’re back to sniping at each other. And it could hardly come at a worse moment as Democrats look to unite behind El-Sayed and hold on to a pivotal Senate seat.

El-Sayed’s campaign is asking Stevens to stay away from all future campaign events in the closing days of the race, two people familiar with the decision, who were granted anonymity to discuss sensitive campaign dynamics, told POLITICO.

She should stay away “as not to distract from the central issues of the campaign,” one of the people said.

The brushoff capped a day of the two lobbing increasingly pointed statements back and forth at each other, frustrating Democrats who say the infighting risks imperiling a state the party can hardly afford to lose as they try to flip control of the Senate.

“I have no idea what the hell Haley Stevens is doing. This is a moment where we have to recognize the stakes,” Michigan state Sen. Mallory McMorrow, who was squeezed out of the Democratic primary by the two other candidates, told CNN earlier on Friday, before El-Sayed issued his response. “People are not talking about ads. They are not talking about Haley Stevens. They are talking about the price of gas and groceries.”

Stevens did not immediately respond to a question on if she will now stay off the trail, and did not respond to an earlier question about whether her appearance was contingent on him apologizing. El-Sayed’s campaign declined to comment.

El-Sayed narrowly defeated Stevens in a bitter and hard-fought primary in August that exposed division over support for Israel. The seat now held by the retiring Democratic Sen. Gary Peters is crucial to Democratic hopes to take control of the Senate.

Stevens and El Sayed have not campaigned together since a post-primary unity breakfast, but appeared on the verge of doing so earlier on Friday.

Stevens initially said she would appear alongside El-Sayed and South Carolina Rep. Jim Clyburn at a church in Detroit on Sunday.

But she said she expected the Democratic nominee to apologize for some of his past comments and condemn an ad run by an outside group supporting him that she described as antisemitic. The ad featured a conversation in a grocery store where a woman told a man they couldn't buy eggs because they had to set aside money for “killing Palestinians.” El-Sayed’s campaign had previously noted he was not affiliated with the ad.

In response, El-Sayed did not touch on the ad or his past comments on Israel, instead saying that his campaign “has always been focused on addressing the most critical challenges Michiganders are facing” and that that focus “is why our people-powered campaign defeated a historic $70 [million] in outside spending from big corporations and AIPAC on behalf of Congresswoman Stevens.”

“So while Congresswoman Stevens seems intent on relitigating a primary that ended three months ago, let’s all focus on the task at hand: that’s beating Mike Rogers,” El-Sayed said.

Privately, Democrats are frustrated — some with Stevens.

“Every Democrat was singing the same tune but now, because one person is tone deaf, the whole choir is thrown off,” said a Michigan Democratic operative who is not directly working for El-Sayed’s campaign.

Some Democrats tried to downplay the fight heading into the final days of the race.

“There’s been a lot of efforts to coalesce Democrats, both from Abdul and from the congresswoman. I think everybody's on the same page of Democrats just beating Mike Rogers," said Joe Tate, a state representative from Detroit who supported Stevens in the primary and is now backing El-Sayed. But he acknowledged that "when you have people that have competed through a primary, I know it can always have those lingering effects."

Former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg urged Democrats to come together to support El-Sayed.

“I've never met somebody who I agree with on 100 percent of everything,” Buttigieg told reporters while campaigning in the state for House candidate Callie Barr on Friday. “But when our health care is at stake, when a chance to have a more affordable everyday life is at stake, the choice is clear: Mike Rogers has disqualified himself.”

Republicans have sought to play up the Democratic divisions. Rogers, in a Friday evening post on X, called Stevens a “courageous leader” and said she was “absolutely right” to call on El-Sayed to apologize.

“I agree with her and my campaign is about being there, showing up and defending these communities and all Michiganders,” Rogers said.

President Donald Trump, at a Friday night rally in Daytona, Florida, had a characteristically blunt message: "If you're Black, or if you're Jewish, and you vote for Abdul, you're not smart,” he said.

Stevens’ comments so close to the pivotal election left some Democrats wondering what her goal was.

“If you are someone with a large platform — whether you are, say, an outgoing member of Congress or an out-of-state streamer — who genuinely wants Abdul El-Sayed to become the next U.S. Senator from the state of Michigan and for Democrats to take back the Senate and put a meaningful check on Donald Trump, before you speak publicly I would implore you to ask yourself ‘is what I’m doing actually helping, or am I just seeking attention?’,” Democratic operative Adam Carlson, who has polled for a pro El-Sayed super PAC, wrote on X.

Sophia Cai contributed to this report.

Over 25% of people lie to get antibiotics—and their lies aren't even good

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A significant number of patients in the US lie to their doctors to get antibiotics they likely don't need, according to two new studies. While that may not be a startling revelation, the studies drills down on the phenomenon, finding that the lies patients tell are generally bad ones—and yet, they work almost all the time.

In the two linked studies published Friday in JAMA Network Open, a team of researchers led by Alistair Thorpe at the University of Utah analyzed online survey data from over 1,000 adults. They found that over a quarter of people reported having lied to a clinician at least once to get an antibiotic prescription for themselves or a child in their care. Patients lied by either exaggerating symptoms, making up symptoms, or—most often—both.

But interestingly, the lies people told to get antibiotics involved making up or exaggerating symptoms of infections that wouldn't be treated with antibiotics. For example, the most common falsehoods were symptoms associated with colds, such as fever, cough, and sore throat. That's despite the fact that colds are caused by viruses, not bacteria—which antibiotics treat.

The Best Fragrance-Free Diapers Of 2026

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Kosamtu/Getty Images

Nobody warns you that having a baby means developing extremely strong opinions about diapers. Here’s one: Pampers AMORE is the fragrance-free diaper to buy in 2026. According to Pampers, it’s Skin Health Alliance-accredited and joins Swaddlers and Pure Protection (which has a vitamin-enriched liner) in the brand’s fragrance-free lineup.

Honestly, your baby’s butt has enough going on without an added scent. Here’s how to decode the labels and find the right fragrance-free diapers for your baby.

What “Fragrance-Free” Actually Means (and Why “Unscented” Isn’t the Same Thing)

You’d think unscented and fragrance-free would mean the same thing. Because, you know, words. But according to the American Academy of Dermatology, unscented products can still contain masking fragrances that cover up other smells. Fragrance-free products, on the other hand, are made without added fragrance.

If you’re shopping for unscented diapers for a child with sensitive or eczema-prone skin, the American Academy of Pediatrics recommends fragrance-free over unscented.

How to Tell If Your Baby Needs a Fragrance-Free Switch

Is it the diaper? The wipes? (All very normal things to Google at 2 a.m.) If you’re searching for the best diapers for eczema-prone skin, start with fragrance-free, especially if you notice:

  • Redness that keeps coming back.
  • A rash that worsens after switching brands.
  • Eczema-prone skin.
  • New, unexplained irritation.

It’s not you (promise). If you’re unsure what’s in a product, the American Academy of Pediatrics suggests erring on the side of fragrance-free. The good news is that it’s getting easier to find. Pampers launched AMORE in March 2026 and announced in June 2026 that all Swaddlers are now fragrance-free, a sign fragrance-free is quickly becoming the norm.

Whether you’re comparing hypoallergenic diapers in 2026 or shopping for fragrance-free diapers for newborns, we ranked these seven on five criteria:

  • Price per diaper

When your baby’s butt is angry, here’s what matters:

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Shoveling snow can be a dangerous winter chore. Icy walkways can cause serious falls, and prolonged exposure to the cold can lead to hypothermia or frostbite. Another safety concern is something called “heart attack snow.”

The name sounds dramatic, but it points to a real concern for people clearing sidewalks and driveways in winter. For those in snowy climates, understanding the risks of heart attack snow is important before picking up a shovel.

Understanding the risks can help prevent a routine chore from becoming a medical emergency.

Why Is Heavy, Wet Heart Attack Snow Dangerous to Shovel?

Heart attack snow is a nickname for heavy, wet snow that’s harder to shovel than dry, powdery snow. The name comes from the strain that lifting this type of snow can place on the heart. According to the National Weather Service, snow with a higher water content weighs more, making each shovelful harder to lift.

The added effort can quickly raise heart rate and blood pressure. Cold temperatures also cause blood vessels to narrow, making the heart work harder. The American Heart Association warns that this combination can trigger a heart attack or sudden cardiac arrest.

The association says the risks are especially concerning for “people who already have cardiovascular risks like a sedentary lifestyle or obesity,” including those with a history of smoking, diabetes, high cholesterol, or high blood pressure. People who have previously suffered a heart attack or stroke also face greater risks.

Even lighter, powdery snow can be dangerous, especially when large amounts require repeated lifting or hours of clearing.

How to Shovel Snow Safely and Reduce the Risk of a Heart Attack

Before heading outside, people should warm up indoors and stretch gently. Dressing in layers helps protect against the cold, while staying hydrated is important even when someone doesn’t feel thirsty.

The American Heart Association recommends pushing snow rather than lifting it whenever possible. When lifting is necessary, smaller loads and frequent breaks can help limit physical strain. A snow blower can also reduce the amount of lifting, although operating one still requires effort.

Slippery surfaces are another concern. “Walking on ice is extremely dangerous,” according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). The agency recommends using rock salt or another de-icing product to keep walkways clear of ice. Sand can also help prevent slipping.

People with heart disease or significant cardiovascular risk factors should have someone else handle snow removal, according to the American Heart Association.

Recognizing the warning signs of a heart attack is equally important. Chest pain or pressure, shortness of breath, dizziness, nausea, cold sweats, or discomfort in the arms, back, or jaw can all signal a serious problem. Symptoms aren’t always severe and can vary from person to person.

Anyone experiencing possible heart attack symptoms should stop shoveling immediately and call 911. The American Heart Association advises against waiting to see whether symptoms go away.

Sources

American Heart Association, “Snow Shoveling, Cold Temperatures Combine for Perfect Storm of Heart Health Hazards”
American Heart Association, “Warning Signs of a Heart Attack”
National Weather Service, “Winter Weather Preparedness”
Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, “Safety Guidelines: During & After a Winter Storm”

The 6 Best Undereye Patches, Tested and Reviewed by Dermatologists and Beauty Editors

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The entire Women's Health team swears by them.

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A powerful psychedelic compound best associated with the Sonoran desert toad (Incilius alvarius) has been recreated in a lab and transformed into a fast-acting nasal spray. A recent clinical trial outlined this week in the Journal of the American Medical Association (JAMA) suggests this spray could help people with treatment resistant depression, without the often less pleasant element of psychedelics. In the study, a single squeeze of the spray eased patients’ depressive symptoms with benefits lasting throughout the eight week trial. 

Crucially, those long-term benefits (which have also been observed in other psychedelic research) didn’t require enduring an all-day mystical trip. Most patients were cleared for release less than two hours after taking the spray. Thankfully, the approach also eliminates the need to fondle a toxic amphibian. That’s potentially a plus both for patients and overharvested toad populations. The finds come amid a resurgence in new research and investment involving psychedelics, supported in part by evolving political attitudes towards the compounds on federal and state levels. 

From toad glands to synthetic nasal spray: a psychedelic journey 

The nasal spray was developed by biotech firm AtaiBeckley. Its  active ingredient is called mebufotenin, which is also known as 5-MeO-DMT. The compound occurs naturally in several places, but it is most famously associated with the secretions of the Sonoran desert toad. For decades, amateur psychonauts have sought out these meaty North American toads for the prominent glands located on the amphibian’s head and hind legs. Squeezing those glands releases a milky secretion (sometimes inaccuracy referred to as venom), which when dried and then vaporized, produces an intense psychedelic experience. Many people who have smoked it describe a profound sense of oneness or ego dissolution, and some even report the sensation of dying. 

Though based on the same compound, the drug in the nasal spray is synthetic and made in a lab. The researchers wanted to see if a controlled, clinical dose of 5-MeO-DMT could deliver some of the compound’s reported therapeutic benefits while keeping the overall treatment brief, especially compared with longer-acting psychedelics like psilocybin. They  limited their study to 193 adults between the ages of 18 to 75 with treatment-resistant depression. The participants, who came from the United States, Europe, and Australia, had each already tried at least two antidepressant treatments without success. By some estimates, around a third of all patients experiencing major depressive disorder also have treatment resistant depression. 

Each participant received nasal spray in either a low (.3mg), medium (8mg), or high (12mg) dose. Neither the patient, nor the clinical staff or researchers involved knew which dose was given. The most intense psychedelic effects wore off relatively quickly, regardless of the dose. Clinicians checked on the patients at regular intervals to determine if they were deemed safe enough to leave the clinic. Among participants who received the 8-milligram dose, 85 percent were ready for discharge within two hours, compared with 78 percent of those who received the 12-milligram dose.

However, the therapeutic effects of the spray appeared to persist far longer.  Patients who received the medium or high dose showed significantly greater improvement than the comparison group as early as two days after treatment, and that advantage held through the end of the eight-week study. The team measured depression with a clinical scale measuring from 0 to 60, with  higher scores indicating more severe symptoms. After four weeks, the 12 mg group had dropped an average of 11.1 points on that score. The 8mg group dropped an even higher 12.5 points while the .3mg group dropped 5.5 points. These findings suggest the nasal spray application could provide some of the same therapeutic effects of other scheduled therapies less less need to have healthcare workers monitoring patients for many hours at a time. 

“In adults with treatment-resistant depression, single doses of intranasal mebufotenin benzoate were well tolerated and produced rapid, robust, and durable reductions in depressive symptoms,” the study authors write. “Intranasal mebufotenin benzoate may improve treatment access and convenience, requiring a shorter monitoring period and fewer clinic visits compared with psilocybin and esketamine, respectively.” 

Psychedelic research gains momentum…and money 

This experimental nasal spray arrives amid a transitionary period for psychedelic research. State governments have shown more interest in the field in recent years, with Oregon and Colorado each launching their own psilocybin programs. Even Texas, a state traditionally associated with a tough on drugs approach, just committed $50 million to research the powerful psychedelic ibogaine. Signs of movement are occurring at the federal level as well. In April, President Donald Trump signed an executive order aimed at accelerating research and FDA approval of psychedelic-based therapies. 

Though it attracted significant attention, the executive order is really more signaling than action. It notably does not lower the bar a therapeutic needs to reach before archived FDA approval. 

Still, pharmaceutical companies are clearly taking note. Earlier this year, Eli Lilly, the largest pharmaceutical company in the world, acquired AtariBeckley for $2.8 billion. AtaiBeckley developed the nasal spray used in the recent JAMA study. 

The post Psychedelic nasal spray treats depression with toad-inspired goo appeared first on Popular Science.

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Mexico City has broken a Guinness World Record during its preparations for this year’s Day of the Dead (Día de los Muertos) celebrations. At nearly 110 feet tall, the colorful sculpture of a female skeleton currently standing in the capital city’s town square (the Zócalo) is officially the world’s tallest La Catrina monument. The roughly 11-story project made from steel and fiberglass required about 140 people to assemble, and overshadows the town of Puerto Vallarta’s 92.3-foot Catrina built back in 2023. The height isn’t the only impressive aspect, either. Her purple dress measures over 88 feet long, while the memorial altar (called an ofrenda) is surrounded by an estimated 70,00 marigold plants grown in the nearby Xochimilco borough.

Observed each year on November 1 and 2, Mexico’s Day of the Dead is famously dedicated to celebrating and honoring deceased family members and loved ones. Historians are divided on its exact origins—while some researchers contend it possesses genuine Indigenous pre-Hispanic roots, others argue the holiday is a more recent rebrand of Spanish traditions intended to promote cultural pride. Regardless of the answer, Day of the Dead has evolved into one of the country’s most popular and iconic observances. Every year, celebrants often wear skeleton-themed costumes and construct ofrendas, while families and friends gather to fondly recall the departed.

The Catrina will remain erected until November 2. Credit: Daniel Cardenas / Anadolu via Getty Images Anadolu

Apart from its size, Mexico City’s record-breaking sculpture is a relatively traditional example of La Catrina, one of Day of the Dead’s most recognizable figures. It’s also much easier to trace La Catrina’s history compared to the holiday’s origins. The figure is based on early 20th century Mexican illustrator José Guadalupe Posada’s most famous work, “La Calavera Catrina” ( or “The Elegant Skull”). Posada’s illustration was released posthumously, and depicts a woman’s skeleton dressed in stylish clothing. Art historians widely view “La Calavera Catrina” as a critique of upper-class Mexican women who attempted to mirror European fashions of the era. Posada’s piece was later expanded upon by the muralist Diego Rivera in 1946-1947. 

Similarly inspired iconography has since become a mainstay of Day of the Dead celebrations. La Catrina sculptures like the new record breaker in Mexico City are frequently surrounded by marigolds. Also known as cempasúchil, the flowers are traditionally believed to help guide spirits home with their scent and colorful petals.

Like life itself, Mexico City’s building-sized La Catrina is only temporary. Admirers will be able to visit the installation and its altar only until November 2.
