By Craig Risi

Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinical psychologists, they can represent something more sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders.

A new report suggests that the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up between 1990 and 2023—by a whopping 71%. By 2023, there were an estimated 18.1 million people living with one of these two disorders.

What’s worse is that the number is likely to be much higher than that once we factor in other eating disorders. And, disturbingly, those in the field are already starting to see GLP-1 weight-loss drugs exacerbating the issue.

Cheri Levinson is a clinical psychologist at the University of Louisville in Kentucky who has specialized in eating disorders for almost 20 years. She says she’s now seeing more patients with more severe symptoms than at any other time in her career, and that many of them are misusing GLP-1s. “This is the worst I’ve seen things,” she says.

Eating disorders are mental health conditions in which people’s eating behaviors impact their health. They include anorexia nervosa (defined by a fear of weight gain), bulimia nervosa (in which people binge and purge), binge eating disorder, and others.

It’s thought that some people have a genetic predisposition to certain eating disorders, but that some external factor is what triggers their onset. That might be stress, or social pressure to be skinny. Often, simply restricting food intake can trigger an eating disorder, says Levinson.

Eating disorders—along with the pressure to look thin—were already prevalent in the 1990s (the heroin chic era springs to mind). To find out how things might have changed since then, Ruhai Bai at the Children’s Hospital of Nanjing Medical University in China and his colleagues pulled together data from studies and surveys conducted on populations around the world. The team used statistical models to fill in any data gaps.

That research allowed the researchers to estimate the annual incidence of two eating disorders—anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa—for each of 204 countries and territories between 1990 and 2023. They also estimated the impact those disorders had on global health by calculating their disability-adjusted life years (one DALY represents a year of healthy life lost).

The findings, published in JAMA Psychiatry on Wednesday, are unsettling. Between 1990 and 2023, there was a 71% increase in the incidence of those two eating disorders, the team found. Those conditions had a huge impact on global health, too. In 2023, they “cost the world the equivalent of about 3.83 million years of healthy life,” says Bai.

Bai and his colleagues expect these figures to continue to rise. In 2023, an estimated 18.1 million people had either anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa; by 2050, the team predict, the two conditions could affect 21.8 million people.

It gets worse. Those two conditions represent less than half of all eating disorders, says Levinson, who was not involved in Bai’s study. The true impact of these disorders is “probably three times the rate that they’re reporting,” she says.

Bai and his colleagues didn’t explore the reasons for this rise (although Bai points out that it can’t be entirely explained by population growth). So how did we get here?

Social media may have played a role. “Social media comparisons, influencers, challenges, diet trends can all be precipitating … events for an eating disorder,” says Cynthia Bulik, a clinical psychologist at the University of North Carolina, who was not involved in the new study. Bulik has been studying and treating eating disorders since the early 1980s. “Between 1990 and 2023 there has clearly been an upsurge of those types of environmental exposures,” she says.

Many social media users are exposed to filtered and modified images of skinny influencers who may have undergone cosmetic surgery or extreme dieting and who are promoting “thinspiration” or “fitspiration” content. But users with eating disorders appear to be more likely to engage with this content—and to be fed more of it as a result.

When researchers at the University of Melbourne investigated this phenomenon a couple of years ago, they found that the algorithms of TikTok users with eating disorders delivered 4343% more “toxic eating disorder videos” than those of users who didn’t have the disorders. They also got more content focused on dieting or exercising. Many were fed over 1000 problematic videos per month. Bulik describes social media as “quicksand” for people with, or at risk of, eating disorders.

The covid-19 pandemic—and the social isolation that accompanied lockdowns—also appears to have contributed to the rise in eating disorders. Research conducted in 2021 found that the number of people hospitalized with eating disorders in the US doubled between 2018 and 2020.

Researchers don’t yet have good data on how trends in eating disorders might have progressed since 2023. But anecdotally, things seem to be getting even worse. “There are more eating disorders, more severe eating disorders… and we’re seeing earlier ages of onset,” says Levinson. “We’re seeing eight- and nine-year-olds with eating disorders.”

“From what I’m hearing and seeing, I am not going to be surprised if that number isn’t at least doubling again,” she adds.

Levinson particularly worries about the impact of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which have exploded in popularity in recent years. A Gallup survey conducted earlier this year suggested that 15% of Americans had used one of these drugs for weight loss. And they’re becoming more accessible—drug companies are working on new formulations that are both easier to take and more effective, and many people can buy versions of these drugs from compound pharmacies or dubious online sources.

In a small study published earlier this year, Levinson and her colleagues found that 30% of people with eating disorders had used GLP-1 drugs. The drugs might benefit some of those people. But 10% appeared to have misused the drugs. And Levinson thinks that figure is also an underestimate.

Levinson says she and her colleagues have seen children develop eating disorders after being prescribed GLP-1s for medical reasons. They’ve seen others—even children as young as 11—buy versions of these drugs over TikTok.

“We’re very concerned that GLPs will lead to even faster and more extreme weight loss in people with anorexia nervosa, and that patients will come to us in even more advanced stages of illness that become difficult to reverse,” says Bulik.

Both Bulik and Levinson are continuing to study the impacts of these drugs in eating disorders. In the meantime, both urge medical professionals to consider the risks GLP-1s might pose to people with those disorders. Bulik contributed to a set of research and clinical guidelines, published earlier this month, that recommends doctors screen for eating disorders before prescribing the drugs, for example. The guidelines also recommend more patient education and more robust monitoring of people who take GLP-1s.

If you think you or someone you know might have an eating disorder, you can find information and support at the National Eating Disorders Association.

Opinion: How Congress can fix expert advisory committees

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In September, Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. fired the two vice chairs of the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force. This move was both unsurprising and troubling. The task force is a critical advisory body to HHS, reviewing scientific evidence to provide recommendations on whether private health insurers should be required to cover services like cancer screenings at no cost to patients. Congress created the task force as part of the Affordable Care Act and require by law that it be staffed by people with “appropriate expertise,” and “to the extent practicable, not subject to political pressure.”

These firings are the latest chapter in a vast project of eliminating, ignoring, and replacing scientific advisory committees. In 2025, the Trump administration terminated 151 advisory committees at scientific government agencies, including 75 in HHS. It has altered or ignored those that are left. In 2025, Kennedy fired the entire Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices, which provides public recommendations on vaccines. His replacements are, to varying degrees, sympathetic to his unscientific views, and a majority lack substantial expertise in vaccines and immunology.

The 12 strangest science stories we can’t stop thinking about

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
A male Amazon river dolphin (Inia geoffrensis) urinating into the air

There are some stories that stay with you forever, the big scientific moments that change the course of history. These are not those moments – but we can’t stop thinking about them anyway.

Push my button

Surgeon Stuart Meloy was performing a pain-relief operation on a woman’s spine when she started exclaiming emphatically. “You’re going to have to teach my husband to do that,” the woman said. This inspired Meloy to invent an implant to help women who are unable to achieve orgasms in the usual way. New Scientist broke the news in 2001, sparking a frenzy of worldwide media excitement. But 13 years later, Meloy was still struggling to raise funds for a full clinical trial, and the “orgasmatron” never reached a climax.

Monkey pleasure

A long-tailed macaque Shutterstock/fiz_zero

We aren’t the only primates capable of coming up with creative ways to pleasure ourselves. In a study of long-tailed macaques (Macaca fascicularis) in Bali, Indonesia, Camilla Cenni at the University of Lethbridge in Alberta, Canada, and her colleagues filmed hundreds of examples of male and female monkeys using stone tools as sex toys, rubbing or tapping them on their genitals. According to Cyril Grueter at the University of Western Australia, the Balinese monkeys get plenty of food handouts from humans, and this frees up time for sexual experimentation. But some monkeys in other places have unusual kinks, too: another study reported a wild macaque in Japan rubbing its genitals on the back of a sika deer.

Advertisement

Head transplants

In 2015, surgeon Sergio Canavero announced plans to carry out the first human head transplant, intended to extend the lives of people with muscle and nerve degeneration or widespread cancer. He claimed his technique could overcome technical hurdles, including how to fuse the spinal cord and prevent the body’s immune system from rejecting the head. In the following two years, Canavero published animal experiments supposedly backing up his methods, but other surgeons remained highly sceptical. A human head transplant never did happen, but Canavero has reportedly moved on to an even bolder ambition: transplanting a brain into a non-sentient clone.

Rats driving

Have you ever wondered if a rat can drive a car? Kelly Lambert at the University of Richmond in Virginia has. She and her colleagues built a tiny car with three copper bars that would steer it left, forward or right. Sure enough, 18 rats learned to drive towards food rewards. They even found it relaxing, as measurements of their stress hormones indicated. Lambert proposed that driving challenges could replace traditional maze tests when using rat models to study neuropsychiatric conditions.

The sniff test

Humans are more similar to rats than we like to think. In a psychology experiment, Noam Sobel and his colleagues at the Weizmann Institute of Science in Rehovot, Israel, surreptitiously filmed 153 volunteers. After shaking hands with someone of the same sex, people smelled their shaking hand more than twice as much as they did before the handshake. Puzzlingly, after shaking hands with someone of the opposite sex, people were more likely to sniff their non-shaking hand. The team suspected that humans use chemical signals all the time without being aware of it.

Urine fountains

Communication through scent is, of course, widespread in mammals, but some never-before-seen behaviour in dolphins took biologists by surprise in 2025. While studying Amazon river dolphins (Inia geoffrensis), or botos, in Brazil, Claryana Araújo-Wang at CetAsia Research Group in Ontario, Canada, saw a male “flip his belly up out of the water, expose his penis and then proceed to urinate into the air”, she told New Scientist. They saw dolphins do this a total of 36 times, and most of the time another male was nearby and would pursue the stream of urine. The purpose of this behaviour hasn’t yet been established, but hormones in urine might signal a male’s social position or physical condition, said Araújo-Wang.

Read more

Orcas have begun wearing salmon hats again – and we may soon know why

Giving the finger

As for human modes of communication, many of us struggle to put our smartphones down – so the last thing we’d want is for them to crawl across the table towards us. Nevertheless, Marc Teyssier at the University of Paris-Saclay in France and his colleagues gave phones this ability in 2018, with a robotic appendage dubbed the MobiLimb. It plugs into a device’s USB port and, for some reason, looks eerily like a human finger, giving it a spooky quality reminiscent of Thing from The Addams Family. The MobiLimb can stroke your wrist, wave, wag like a tail or, most disconcertingly, drag the phone around. Thankfully, the idea hasn’t taken off.

Horror frog

A taxidermied male hairy frog Gustavocarra/CC BY-SA 4.0

If you find the thought of a phone with a finger horrifying, wait until you hear about the nightmare-inducing hairy frog Trichobatrachus robustus. In 2008, researchers reported that these frogs can break their own bones to produce claws that burst through their skin. David Blackburn at Harvard University’s Museum of Comparative Zoology thought these weapons were for defence, but two years later, another team argued that they were used in fights between males – and that the frog’s unusual hairs were a kind of armour to protect its vulnerable parts from sharp claws.

Corpse shrinking

In 1957, when New Scientist was 1 year old, we reported on a new method for shrinking an adult human body to the size of a baby. At an exhibition of inventors in Winnipeg, Canada, the unnamed inventor of this process claimed it would make disposing of dead bodies simpler, as they could be slid into underground tubes and take up less space. The method involved applying high pressure to the corpse and squeezing out fluids so its flesh could be compressed into a mould. “All this is, of course, highly ingenious but it is uncertain why the inventor thinks that his newly proposed form of burial is in any way better than the current and very popular method of cremation,” the writer mused.

Read more

The brain has its own microbiome. Here’s what it means for your health

Transient anus

The warty comb jelly has an anus that comes and goes Andrey Nekrasov/imageBROKER.com​/Alamy

We may take it for granted, but the anus, which allows food to pass through the body, was an evolutionary breakthrough, both figuratively and literally. The earliest animals, like jellyfish today, had only one gut opening, where food went in and waste came out. In 2019, scientists discovered that the warty comb jelly (Mnemiopsis leidyi, pictured top) forms an anal opening when it needs to defecate, but it vanishes straight afterwards. This, they suggested, could represent how the anus evolved in the first place.

Fart science

Flatus – gas expelled from the anus – has been a topic of endless fascination for many readers (and journalists). In 2001, we featured the work of Michael Levitt at the Veterans Administration Medical Center in Minneapolis, a leading authority on the subject. He developed a “Toot Trapper” that reduces odours by trapping certain gases using activated charcoal. We also reported on a device for monitoring gas emissions from dogs, used to test dog snacks that were supposed to reduce flatulence. In 2016, researchers in Australia worked out which foods cause the smelliest farts by mixing faeces with different foods. They recommended eating less protein and more slow-release carbohydrates to minimise hydrogen sulphide production. In 2022, Nagarajan Kannan at the Mayo Clinic in Rochester, Minnesota, and his colleagues worked out that some poos float because of gas from gut microbes, confirming a hypothesis first put forward by Levitt 50 years earlier.

Brew your own

Even stranger things go on inside some people’s intestines, as we found out in 2019. A 46-year-old man started experiencing unexplained mental fogginess, dizziness and memory loss, and had to give up his job. Later, doctors found high levels of alcohol in his blood when he hadn’t been drinking. Eventually, they realised his gut contained high levels of Saccharomyces cerevisiae – brewer’s yeast – which got him drunk any time he ate a lot of carbohydrates. This rare condition, known as auto brewery syndrome, had been triggered when the man took antibiotics for a thumb injury, which disrupted his gut microbiome. This year, a study reported that another man with this condition fully recovered after getting a transplant of faeces from a healthy donor. Both articles were written by Alice Klein, who we also have to thank for the monkey sex toys, car-driving rats, smelly farts and floating poos. Thanks, Alice!

This bike will stop your kid’s YouTube video if they’re not pedaling

📅 Oct 30, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Kids watch a lot of YouTube. And that generally means a lot of sedentary, butt-in-seat time.

But what if they had to pedal to keep the content coming?

That’s the question entrepreneur couple Arnav Dalmia and Shivani Jain are asking with their new company/product Tovie.

Described as a “sensory-friendly bike that helps kids use movement to self-regulate,” Tovie is a peddling machine where kids sit on whatever chair/couch/etc is comfortable and get their feet going. (If this sounds familiar: Yes, it’s basically like those under-desk peddlers for office workers. More on that later.)

The reason Tovie piqued our interest is Dalmia and Jain’s proprietary “Pedal-to-Play” system, which “makes movement the controller,” they say. It works by connecting Tovie to a kid’s device via Bluetooth, and whenever they stop pedaling, the YouTube video they’re watching (or the video game they’re playing) stops working.

The goal is to keep kids moving their bodies but still allowing them screen time.

“Studies done by researchers have found that movement in general is a tool for self-regulation of the hormones in their bodies, to help the nervous system calm down,” Dalmia told Inc. “It helps get the excess energy out before they have a big meltdown.”

He added that with Tovie, he and Jain feel they are “creating a whole new category of children’s fitness.”

“People think that children can get exercise while outside playing, but not all kids are the same, and they have different circumstances. In winter, it’s too cold outside. If parents are busy preparing dinner, they can’t watch their kids playing outside,” he added.

This isn’t Dalmia and Jain’s first stationary exercise product. In 2013, they debuted Cubii, an under-desk elliptical, and to bring it to life, they raised almost $300,000 on Kickstarter.

They’re producing Tovie the same way. This time, the Kickstarter goal is only $50K, and with nine days left in the campaign, they’re about to cross that threshold thanks to just over 100 backers.

Once they’ve shipped the initial models to Kickstarter supporters, the couple hope to put Tovie in big-box stores like Target and Walmart–and then keep developing products.

“The Tovie bike will be the first of many bikes focused on children’s health,” Dalmia told Inc. “Being a dad myself, I know how important physical movement is. The goal is to encourage movement and get kids started with fitness to start good lifelong habits.”

Does ADHD Worsen With Age? It Depends.

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Not too long ago, ADHD was largely thought of as a childhood condition that diminished in adulthood. Today, we understand that ADHD is a lifelong condition. What’s less clear: How does ADHD change across the lifespan, if at all?

This is a question that professor and clinical psychologist Margaret Sibley, Ph.D., addressed during her ADDitude webinar, “How ADHD Symptoms & Impairments Change with Age.”

The answer is still coming into focus but one central truth is emerging: There isn’t a single ADHD experience. Whether ADHD worsens or improves over time for someone depends on multiple factors, from when symptoms and impairments first become meaningful to how sensitive the individual’s ADHD is to their environment.

Sibley’s take-home from the research? “It depends on who you are.”

The Symptom vs. Impairment Question

The question of how ADHD changes over time — whether it gets better or worse for people — is tricky, in part, because of the symptom vs. impairment question. That is, someone may show symptoms of ADHD, but the degree to which those symptoms impact their life may shift with age, overall health, and myriad other circumstances.

[Your Free Guide to ADHD in Older Adults]

“Not everyone who has symptoms is impaired,” Sibley said.

From major life transitions (like perimenopause and its accompanying impacts on cognition), environmental demands (e.g., a job that strains executive functioning) to coping skills, what’s going on in someone’s life “may influence whether a person actually experiences problems because of their symptoms,” Sibley said.

ADHD Waxes and Wanes

The question of whether ADHD worsens or improves over time suggests that the condition is linear. For many people, this is not the case.

“It doesn’t get better or get worse. But depending on what’s happening in your life, you may have good years and not-good years,” Sibley said, whose research found that higher environmental demands are associated with “better-functioning ADHD.”

[Read: ADHD’s Vanishing and Reappearing Act]

What Predicts If ADHD Gets Better or Worse?

If you’ve experienced symptoms and impairments since childhood, your symptoms will likely get better, while impairments may or may not worsen.

If your symptoms first began to impact you in adulthood — perhaps triggered by aging, hormonal fluctuations, or other changes — research suggests that your symptoms and impairments may worsen over time.

If you’re part of the subgroup of people whose ADHD fluctuates from year to year, you may have a form of ADHD that is environmentally sensitive.

Other factors that generally predict a worse course of ADHD, according to research, include but are not limited to the following:

  • More severe ADHD at baseline
  • Family adversity
  • Mood problems and emotional dysregulation issues

As far as what can make ADHD better? Sibley suggests the following steps:

  • Be strategic about your environment.
  • Develop coping skills to positively experience and manage your symptoms.
  • Prepare for major life transitions.
  • Learn skills to mitigate the impact of symptoms on impairment (e.g., externalizing information, adhering to routines, better managing time)

To learn more about how ADHD changes across time, watch the full webinar at additu.de/102926

Birds can get jet lag on long, fast flights and then fly off course

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
A Manx shearwater in flight low over the sea, near Wales Nature Picture Library / Alamy Stock Photo

You don’t need a jet plane to get jet lag – even feathers will do. At least one species of bird gets jet lag, in the sense that its body clock can diverge from local time by more than an hour on fast flights across the Atlantic, as revealed by navigational errors.

“We’ve slightly shied away from using the term jet lag itself, because people associate that with the health impacts of that clock shift, rather than the clock shift itself. But it’s exactly the same process that causes jet lag in people,” says Patrick Lewin at the University of Oxford. “It’s having these physiological and behavioural effects, where the birds are mistiming their behaviours.”

It is likely that other long-distance fliers, possibly including some insects and bats as well as birds, also get jet lag in the clock-shift sense, says Lewin.

Advertisement

Read more

Birds wearing tiny trackers reveal migration routes in amazing detail

Manx shearwaters are small seabirds that fly vast distances, using a technique called dynamic soaring to exploit wind gradients near the sea surface. “They’ve evolved this niche of being really amazing long-distance travellers,” says Lewin.

They breed on islands around the UK and Ireland. While parents are raising a chick, they may fly more than 1000 kilometres westwards to reach rich fishing grounds in the mid-Atlantic. Once they have food, they fly back in a fairly straight line, diverting only to skirt around landmasses such as Ireland.

How do they achieve these navigational feats? While some birds can sense Earth’s magnetic field, Manx shearwaters don’t appear to. Lewin’s team has confirmed this by attaching small magnets to the birds that should disrupt geomagnetic navigation. They had no effect.

Free newsletter

Sign up to The Daily

Sign up to newsletter

Instead, it appears that shearwaters rely on a sun compass – if you know what time it is, you can maintain a course by adjusting your orientation relative to the sun. Many animals, from insects and fish to turtles and birds, have been shown to do this. If their internal clocks are reset using artificial light and darkness, they head in the wrong direction.

“We realised that there might be a kind of natural experiment where the birds were manipulating their own clocks,” says Lewin.

For instance, at 6am, a Manx shearwater wanting to fly eastwards back to its nest needs to head sunwards. At noon, it needs to fly at right angles to the sun, which will be due south. But if a shearwater travels 15° of longitude eastwards, the local time will be 1pm, while its internal clock will still register the time as noon, so the sun will no longer be due south. This means birds flying rapidly east should veer slightly off course in a clockwise direction over the course of day, in the absence of other cues.

Read more

Australian moths use the stars as a compass on 1000-km migrations

Other researchers have been attaching GPS trackers to Manx shearwaters for decades, so the paths taken on nearly 4000 foraging trips are known. Sure enough, when Lewin and his colleagues analysed these flight tracks, they found birds on rapid return flights tended to deviate clockwise when out of sight of land.

The team considered other potential explanations, from the Coriolis effect to winds. “The one thing that best predicted the observed changes in shearwaters’ orientation was always this sun compass effect,” says Lewin.

The effect is small. After a 1000 km flight, a bird would have to fly only an extra 5 km to reach its colony, which, for such efficient long-distance fliers, is probably negligible, Lewin says. This might explain why Manx shearwaters haven’t evolved some mechanism for avoiding jet lag, he says.

Flight Paths review: Why understanding migration may save bird species

Solving the mysteries of bird migration with technology not only unlocks unexpected secrets but is crucial to conservation efforts, says Rebecca Heisman in her fascinating first book

“What is good about the study is that they analyse a lot of tracks, making for robust data to test different models,” says Ken Cheng at Macquarie University in Sydney.

While other species may get this form of jet lag, too, long-distance flights by birds are typically done during migrations, and migrations usually involve mainly north-south travel, says Cheng, so jet lag won’t be much of a problem.

However, near the poles, the travel distance required to cross 15° or more of longitude becomes so short that many animals might be affected, says Lewin, perhaps even including polar bears or Arctic foxes travelling on sea ice.

“Near-polar species may be quite strongly affected,” he says. “Now, it all gets a bit complicated because you have 24-hour daylight and 24-hour darkness in parts of the year, so circadian rhythms break down generally in some species.”

Journal Reference:

Science DOI: 10.1126/science.adz2542

The Lat Training Guide For Building a Stronger and Wider Back

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Most lifters want lats for one reason:  the V-taper. Well-developed lats give your upper body that powerful look from the front, side, and back, and yes, they make T-shirts fit better.

Besides looking awesome, the lats keep your shoulders stable, support better upper-body posture, and connect your arms to your torso, improving form during heavy lifts. Strong lats improve pulling mechanics, create a stronger pressing setup, and help your upper back stay in a better position during squats and deadlifts.

Here we’ll go over all things lats, including:

  • Lat anatomy and movement
  • Why they matter
  • What the lats need for size, strength, and better movement
  • The best ways to train them
  • How to program sets, reps, frequency, and weekly volume
  • Common lat training mistakes
  • Lat training myths
  • Two sample lat workouts

By the end, you’ll know how to train your lats for a stronger back, better pulls, healthier shoulders, and an upper body that will fill out your T-shirts.

Lat Anatomy

The latissimus dorsi—or lats for short—are the body’s widest muscles and cover much of the back. They’re broad, flat, and fan-shaped, which is why well-developed lats help create that V-Taper look.

Because of their size, the lats attach to the

  • Spine
  • Pelvis
  • Shoulder blades
  • Humerus

Because they attach to the upper arm and spine, they influence both shoulder movement and posture.

Their main movements are:

  • Shoulder adduction: When you pull your arm down toward your side.
  • Shoulder extension: When you pull your arm behind your body.
  • Shoulder internal rotation: When the inside of your shoulder joint brings your forearm and hand closer to you.

They also help with scapular depression, which pulls the shoulders down away from your ears and is an important part of good pulling form. Train those actions well, and you’ll build a wider back, stronger pulls, and better control from your shoulders through your hips.

 

Why They Matter, Besides the Obvious

We all know that lats are the engine behind rows, pull-ups, and chin-ups, but some don’t realize the vital role they play beyond them.

During the deadlift, strong, engaged lats help keep the bar close to your body as you pull. When the bar drifts away, the lift gets harder, and your lower back gets unhappy.

Your lats matter during the bench press, too. When engaged, they help create a stable base to press from, improve your pressing path, and transfer force from your leg drive to your upper body.

That is why strong lats aren’t just a “back day” thing: they set the table for better pressing form.

Pulling your lats down and back creates upper-body tightness for barbell back squat. Doing so helps stabilize the bar and keeps you in a stronger, more upright position instead of folding forward into a good morning.

The lats also matter for shoulder health and posture because they influence shoulder position and upper-body alignment. When the lats are balanced in length, they let the shoulder blades rest flat, and the shoulders open back naturally. This supports better posture.

And yes, they matter outside the gym, too. Pulling open heavy doors, climbing, carrying objects close to your body, and lifting things from the floor all involve the lats. Bigger lats create the look, but stronger lats help you pull, brace, carry, and move well.

What the Lats Need

Because the lats are large and their muscle fibers run in different directions, train them through several pulling paths. You need vertical pulling, horizontal pulling, straight-arm work, loaded stretches, and good form to keep the tension where it belongs.

  • Start Vertical: Pull-ups and chin-ups remain the gold standard, while lat pulldowns let you use multiple grips and body positions to target the lats from different angles to increase volume for size and strength.
  • Rows That Train The Lats: The Underhand dumbbell row is a great example. Use an underhand grip and pull the dumbbell back in an arc, keeping your elbow close to your torso, starting below your shoulder and ending near the outside of your hip. This shifts the focus from the upper back to the lats.
  • Straight-Arm Work: Straight-arm pulldowns and their variations are as close as you’ll get to a lat isolation exercise. They’re useful because they reduce bicep involvement, making them great for lifters who struggle to feel their lats.
  • Unilateral Variations: Bilateral variations let you use more load but often hide muscle imbalances between sides. Strengthening these imbalances improves performance and strength on bilateral lifts and enhances lat development between sides.

Lat Consideration for Growth

You know the exercises, but that’s only part of it. For maximum growth and strength, keep these four things in mind.

  • Feel the Stretch: All muscles, including the lats, respond well to the loaded stretch because you’ll work through a full range of motion and the muscle stays under tension longer. Performing chin-ups and pull-ups from a dead hang will have your lats saying thank you.
  • Don’t Go Crazy:  Good form, full range of motion, and shoulder mobility all matter. Train the lats hard, but keep the reps clean. Big lats are great, but big lats that move well are better.
  • Full Range of Motion: Own both ends of the rep. Reach into the stretch under control, then finish by pulling the elbows down, back, or toward the hips, depending on the exercise. Cutting the ROM short changes your gains.

Progressive Overload: Add load, reps, sets, range of motion, or better control over time. But don’t sacrifice lat tension, shoulder position, or form to move more weight.

Sets, Reps, and Frequency

Here are general recommendations that work for most lifters most of the time.

Lat Strength

Use sets of three to six reps on heavier pulls. Good options include weighted pull-ups, heavy chin-ups, and lat pulldowns. Rest 2 to 3 minutes between sets, and keep the reps clean.

Lat Size

Use sets of eight to 15 reps on pulldowns, rows, dumbbell rows to the hip, seated machine rows, or cable rows. This is your bread-and-butter hypertrophy work. Use a full range of motion, control the stretch, and finish each rep by pulling the elbows toward the ribs or hips, depending on the exercise.

For a Better Connection

Use sets of 12 to 20 reps on straight-arm pulldowns, kneeling straight-arm pulldowns, cable pullovers, and band straight-arm pulldowns. These are great for lifters who struggle to feel their lats because these exercises isolate them.

For Deadlift and Big-Lift Carryover

Straight-arm pulldown variations are useful before deadlifting because engaging the lats helps maintain a neutral spine and keep the bar close. One or two sets of 10 to 15 reps work well as a primer.

Training Frequency

Most lifters can train the lats directly two to three times per week.

Think of 12 to 16 direct lat sets per week as your weekly target. How you divide those sets depends on your goal.

If strength is the priority, spend more sets in the 3 to 6 rep range with weighted pull-ups, heavy pulldowns, chin-ups, and rows. If size is the goal, focus more work in the 8 to 15 rep range with pulldowns, rows, single-arm work, and machine or cable variations. If you struggle to feel your lats, include 12 to 20 rep sets of straight-arm pulldowns, pullovers, or band work.

Here’s an example of how to organize your weekly volume. 12 weekly sets might include four heavier sets, six muscle-building sets, and two higher-rep straight-arm sets. That gives you a fantastic combination of strength, size, and lat connection.

Common Lat Training Mistakes

Using too much weight you cannot control might stroke the ego, but it does nothing for your gains. Here are a few more mistakes to watch out for to get the most out of your lat training.

Arm Heavy

Your hands hold the bar, but your elbows should lead the movement. When lifters focus only on pulling with their hands, chin-ups, pulldowns, and rows often turn into biceps-heavy exercises with a side order of forearm fatigue.

Fix: Drive your elbows down during pulldowns, pull your elbows toward your hips during rows, and tuck your shoulder blades into your back pocket for chin-ups.

Missing The Stretch

Many lifters cut the top of pulldowns, pull-ups, and straight-arm exercises short. That leaves a lot of potential lat tension on the table, because the lats love a loaded stretch.

Fix: Reach overhead or forward under control, keep the ribs down, and feel the stretch without dumping on the shoulder joint. The goal is to control a stretch, not just hang through the connective tissue.

Rib Flare

When the ribs flare, the lower back often arches, turning a lat exercise into a lower-back exercise. This can happen during pulldowns, pullovers, and straight-arm work when someone tries to create range they don’t own.

Fix: Keep the ribs stacked over the pelvis by breathing out and bracing the core on top of that. This gives the lats a better chance to lengthen and shorten without the lower back taking over.

Too Much Upper Traps

If your shoulders shrug toward your ears during rows or pulldowns, your upper traps dominate. Doing so takes the focus off the lats and can lead to upper trap and neck pain.

Fix: Remember to keep your shoulders away from your ears and your chest up. You do this by keeping your neck long.

4 Common Lat Training Mistakes That Ruin Back Gains

Lat training has its share of gym myths because everyone wants a wider back. Some advice sounds good until you realize it ignores range of motion, elbow path, body position, and the fact that your biceps will love to steal the show if you let them.

Wide Grip Builds Wide Lats

Wide-grip pulldowns and pull-ups are great, but they aren’t magic—and they don’t build wide lats. A wide grip doesn’t automatically increase lat tension. In fact, it often shortens your range of motion, limits the load, and shifts stress to the shoulders and upper back for many lifters. Shoulder-width, medium, and neutral grips offer a longer ROM, and you can lift more weight.

All You Need Is Pull-Ups

Pull-ups and chin-ups are gold-standard lat builders—but the lats need more. The problem is that pull-ups lock you into a single line of pull. Because the lats are large and wide, with muscle fibers running in different directions, dumbbell and machine variations let you adjust your body position and grips to change the line of pull and target muscle fibers that chin-ups miss.

Rows Are Only For The Upper Back

Rows hammer the upper back, but they can also hit the lats with the right setup, grip, and elbow path. Rowing high with flared elbows shifts more attention to the traps, rear delts, and upper back. Rowing back toward the hip with your elbow close gives the lats more work.

Lats Are Only For Back Day

They assist with deadlifts, squats, bench press setup, posture, and shoulder control. So yes, train them for width. But don’t forget they also help you lift better when no one is admiring your V-taper.

Two Sample Lat-Focused Workouts

The lats don’t need a separate “back day or bust” approach. You can train them hard during an upper-body session or weave them into a full-body workout.

Here are two examples.

 

WORKOUT 1: UPPER-BODY LAT-FOCUSED WORKOUT

Goal: Build lat size and strength while still training the chest, shoulders, upper back, and arms.

  • 1A. Chin-up, Pull-Up, or Lat Pulldown: 4 sets, 6-12 reps
  • 1B. Dumbbell Bench Press: 3 sets, 6-12 reps
  • 2A. Underhand Dumbbell Row: 3 sets, 12-15 reps per side
  • 2B. Dumbbell Overhead Press: 3 sets, 6-12 reps
  • 3A. Chest-Supported Row: 3 sets, 10-15 reps
  • 3B. Biceps Curl Variation: 3 sets, 10-15 reps

WORKOUT 2: FULL-BODY LAT WORKOUT

Goal: Train the lats as part of a full-body workout.

  • 1A. Band-Resisted RDL: 3 sets, 6-8 reps
  • 1B. Dumbbell Bench Press: 3 sets, 6-12 reps
  • 2A. Half-Kneeling Single-Arm Lat Pulldowns: 3 sets, 12-15 reps per side
  • 2B. Goblet Split Squat: 3 sets, 6-12 reps per side
  • 3A. Unilateral Cable Row: 2-3 sets, 15 reps per side
  • 3B. Back Extension: 2-3 sets, 12-15 reps
  • 3C. Dumbbell Pullover: 2-3 sets, 10-15 reps

The Final Set

The winning formula is this: Train the lats with vertical pulls, rows, straight-arm work, single-arm variations, and exercises that challenge the stretch position. Keep the ribs down, control the shoulders, pull toward the hips to engage more lats, and stop letting your biceps, traps, and lower back take over.

Remember to lock the lats in during your squat, bench, and deadlift because they are the difference between a sloppy rep and a clean one. Use enough volume to grow and enough weight to build muscle and strength, but never shortchange the range of motion.

If you do it right, you’ll need to buy larger shirts.

The Ongoing Crisis in Menopause Care

📅 Oct 29, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Suffering through menopausal hot flashes, night sweats, sleep disturbances, mood shifts, and memory problems? Wondering why your ADHD medication has suddenly become less effective? Considering a visit with your doctor to better understand and treat these deeply disruptive symptoms?

Sadly, your healthcare provider may be less informed than you might hope.

A disturbing but unsurprising analysis, published recently in The Lancet Obstetrics, Gynecology, & Women’s Health, found that many healthcare professionals are ill-equipped to provide responsive, coordinated, evidence-based treatment and support to help women navigate this often-challenging stage of life. It cited inadequate medical training, paltry research, and persistent gaps in knowledge as reasons for the “fragmented care.”1

The analysis of 89 studies on health system responsiveness across the U.S. and several other countries also found that women in marginalized groups disproportionately experienced barriers to care and received limited information about available treatment options by providers.

ADHD and the Menopause Experience

A woman who has gone 12 months without a menstrual period is in menopause. The median age for menopause is 51, though many women with ADHD are known to experience the onset earlier.

[Get This Free Download: Menopause and ADHD]

Declining estrogen levels during perimenopause and menopause are associated with reduced dopamine, leading to worsening ADHD symptoms and emotional dysregulation in many women – even those who had previously managed their ADHD symptoms well with medication, according to a separate study published in the Journal of Attention Disorders.

In surveys conducted over recent years, thousands of women have disclosed to ADDitude how midlife hormonal changes exacerbated their ADHD symptoms and adversely affected every area of their lives, from work performance and sleep to relationships with family and friends. Many said they sought help from providers who had limited knowledge and few clinical studies to consult.

“Perimenopause and menopause play a big role in my inattentive ADHD, and I wish more was known by those who diagnose and treat us,” said Cindy, a ADDitude reader from Santa Clara, California.

“The fact that females are massively underdiagnosed and understudies is horrible but not surprising,” said another ADDitude reader who was diagnosed with ADHD at age 38. “How symptoms may change during perimenopause and menopause, how this affects your home life as a wife and mother, is not talked about enough.”

[Read: Falling Estrogen, Soaring ADHD]

“It’s insane,” said another ADDitude reader, “and nobody is helping us to treat the super storm of symptoms, including brain fog, exhaustion, headaches, and so on.”

Menopausal hormone therapy is considered the most effective treatment for alleviating common symptoms and is often recommended for women experiencing adverse effects of perimenopause. Non-hormonal medications and complementary approaches, including cognitive behavioral therapy, have also shown benefits in symptom management, the Lancet analysis said.

6 ways to make homework less of a battle when your kid has ADHD

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Tonight’s homework is a single worksheet. It’s been 50 minutes. Your kid has sharpened every pencil in the house, visited the bathroom twice, and is now face-down on the floor insisting it’s impossible. You can feel your patience slipping by the second.

If homework battles turn your evenings into a standoff, you’re far from alone, especially if your child has ADHD. About 1 in 9 US kids have been diagnosed with the condition , and the American Academy of Pediatrics says it can profoundly affect a child’s academic achievement. For families managing ADHD, the after-school stretch is often the hardest part of the day.

There’s a reason those hours are so trying. By the time a child with ADHD gets home,  they’ve spent “six or seven hours working harder than their classmates to sit still and focus,” says Michael Morse, MD, a board-certified child and adolescent psychiatrist who directs the ADHD & Anxiety Center at Alvord Baker and is an associate professor of psychiatry at Georgetown University. That effort is used up, and for many kids the medication has worn off too, so “the after-school brain is often a tired brain.”

The resistance usually isn’t a matter of defiance. Executive function, the set of skills needed for getting started, planning and keeping track of steps, develops more slowly in kids with ADHD. That’s why a child can freeze in front of an assignment they’re fully capable of completing, and why the thing that helps is making the first step smaller rather than pushing harder.

Related 6 ways to make mornings smoother when your child has ADHD

Here are six ways Morse says parents can make homework less of a fight for everyone.

1. Don’t start homework the minute they arrive home

The worst time to start homework is the moment your child walks through the door. The school day has already drained the same systems homework demands, so Morse suggests framing the immediate after-school window as a time to refuel. “A snack with some protein comes first, because after-school hunger makes both focus and mood worse,” he says. Movement comes next, whether that’s riding a bike or walking the dog, which burns off the day’s tension and helps many kids with ADHD settle. Make the break predictable with a clear end, and pick a consistent homework time that comes after the break and well before your child is too fried to think.

Most importantly, hold off on screens. “The transition from screens to homework can be particularly painful,” Morse says, so it’s usually easier not to turn them on after school at all.

2. Shrink the first step

“A task feels too big when a child can’t see where it starts,” Morse says. Many kids with ADHD freeze in front of a big assignment because their brain can’t break it into steps on its own, so the task is to shrink the first step until it feels easy. 

Say a book report is due in two weeks. Sit down together and have your child break it into pieces: choose the book, read one chapter, jot notes on the main character. Then put each piece on a calendar so the deadline isn’t, in Morse’s words, “a cliff at the end.” For a single night’s work, 10 math problems can become two sets of five with a break in between. And when your child finishes a piece, say so out loud, since that small win makes the next one easier to start. (Younger kids need the pieces smaller and the check-ins more frequent.)

Related 8 common myths about ADHD—debunked

3. Coach the work instead of enforcing it

“Players usually appreciate coaches who run laps with them,” Morse says. He encourages parents to be “a coach and a model, not an enforcer.” In practice that can look like sitting at the kitchen table with your own work, while your child does theirs. A calm adult nearby helps many kids with ADHD get started and keep going. What a coach doesn’t do is finish the work so it comes back perfect, “because then the teacher never sees what the child can really do,” Morse says. The job is to help plan the night and praise the effort while leaving the outcome up to your child.

4. Offer two choices to sidestep the standoff

When homework turns into a power struggle, handing a little control back to your child can often defuse it. Offer two options you’re happy with either way, like “Math or reading first?” or “Before dinner or after, but before screens.” “A choice gives a child some control,” Morse says, which can take the fight out of the room before it starts. (If homework is still a daily battle no matter what you try, Morse notes a tutor can often take the coaching role with less friction than a parent.)

5. Know when to call it for the night

Some nights, the right move is to stop. “A parent should call it when the homework is no longer teaching anything or when substantial conflict emerges,” Morse says. If your child is in tears, exhausted or stuck on the same problem despite your help, more pushing won’t produce learning. “It mostly produces a worse night and a child who dreads the next one,” he says. When you stop, have your child’s back by sending the teacher a short note about how long they worked and where they got stuck. Most teachers would rather know.

“Your relationship with your child is more important than any assignment,” Morse says, and that relationship is what carries a kid through years of school. Kids with ADHD already hear a lot of criticism, and “if homework turns every evening into a battle, home stops feeling like the place where they’re accepted.”

Related Should homework be banned? What experts and moms say

6. Loop in the teacher when it’s bigger than effort

Some homework problems are too big to solve at the kitchen table. “I’d start advocating when the problem looks bigger than effort,” Morse says. Watch for homework that routinely takes far longer than the teacher intended, work your child understands but can’t get written down or turned in, or nights that keep ending in tears. Those point to a gap between what the assignment asks and what your child can do right now, and pushing harder at home won’t close it. 

Start by telling the teacher how long the work is actually taking. Some kids need more formal support through a 504 plan or an individualized education program, the school plans that put accommodations in writing. This matters especially for girls, Morse says, “whose late-night effort can keep their grades up and hide how much they’re struggling.”

As kids get older, Morse’s advice is to hand over the wheel gradually, with middle school as the moment to watch, when additional teachers and long-term projects arrive just as parents tend to step back. Teach your child to use a planner, check in briefly each evening, and let them take on more as they show they can. The long-term goal is teaching a kid which tasks will always need a system or a helper, because “knowing when to ask for help is one of the most valuable skills a teenager can carry into adult life.”

That need goes well beyond ADHD. Accountability partners and body doubles, “people whose quiet company helps you start and stay on task,” work for nearly everyone. “Not everyone knows to ask,” Morse says.

Studies Show Diet Sodas May Not Be Stalling Weight Loss as Much as Previously Thought

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Artificially sweetened drinks are often demonized based on the theory that they may encourage a greater appetite, or supercharge our sugar cravings, but recent work appears to dispel the bad rap around diet sodas and gaining weight. Now, a two-year clinical trial from the University of Liverpool, England, has provided the greatest insight yet, into the long-term effects of diet drinks on weight loss.

The groundbreaking study, conducted over the course of 104 weeks and published in the British Journal of Nutrition, attempted to build on a similar report in 2023 that also found no proof for the theory that diet sodas can stall weight loss.

Do Diet Drinks Cause Weight Gain or Cravings? What the 2-Year SWITCH Trial Proves

While both studies pitted water against zero and low-calorie drinks that were free of sugar (also known as non-nutritive, or NNS beverages) the continued efforts to find the true value of diet drinks is critical, because sugar sweetened beverages often account for a substantial source of dietary sugar, excess calories, and an increased risk of type 2 diabetes. So, if science can show that NNS drinks do not lead to increased sugar intake or excess appetite, they could instead play a part in a balanced diet. To that end, the Liverpool University study has been described as the longest, most comprehensive trial in this area to date. “This study provides the long‑term clinical evidence that has been missing from international discussions,” explained Professor John Halford, who was lead author on the project.

2-Year Clinical Trial Comparison: Diet Drinks vs. Water

493 overweight or obese adults were entered into a structural weight management program and then split into those who only drank water, and those who consumed a choice of 20 commercially available diet drinks containing aspartame, sucralose, and acesulfame.  Each drink contained less than 2.1 calories per 100ml, with no overall daily calorie cap on drinks. The NNS group were allowed to drink water, but they were asked to drink at least two 330ml servings per day of a diet beverage.

Interestingly, neither group was not blocked from drinking sugary beverages, but they did received coaching to reduce them.

What were the results after 104 weeks?

  •   Weight loss difference between NNS and water groups deemed insignificant
  •   Both groups maintained sustained weight reduction for the full 2 years
  •   Both groups saw reductions in waist size along with improved body composition

The study also found that overall sugar intake decreased slightly greater in those drinking the NNS beverages, seemingly disproving the theory that artificially sweetened drinks encourage sugar cravings, and suggesting the opposite effect may occur.

“These latest findings show that concerns about sweeteners disrupting appetite or causing weight gain are not supported when tested in a rigorous, long-term randomized trial,” said Professor Harrold. “We hope this evidence informs future WHO guidance and public health policy.”

Perimenopause Begins Earlier and Hits Harder for Women with ADHD

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Perimenopause is destabilizing, and even debilitating for many women with ADHD. In an ADDitude survey of nearly 5,000 women, 63% of respondents aged 45 and older said ADHD had the greatest impact on their lives during the menopausal transition.

Fluctuating estrogen levels affect attention, memory, executive function, and emotional regulation. Meanwhile, hot flashes, poor sleep, anxiety, and brain fog place added stress on an already strained nervous system.

Emerging research suggests that women with ADHD may experience perimenopause and its symptoms earlier than their neurotypical peers. In a study published in European Psychiatry, women with ADHD reported their most severe perimenopausal symptoms from ages 35 to 39 — almost a decade younger than women without ADHD. Their symptoms were also relatively more severe, which may help explain the uptick in ADHD diagnoses at this time.1

We cannot yet definitively say that hormonal changes begin earlier in women with ADHD. However, research suggests that significant symptoms of perimenopause may emerge earlier than clinicians expect.

Perimenopause Is Under-Recognized

If your periods, sleep, mood, or ADHD symptoms have changed, do not dismiss them because a doctor says you’re “too young” for menopause. These changes deserve thoughtful medical evaluation.

[Free Webinar Replay: Perimenopause in Women with ADHD]

In a large study published in the journal Menopause, one in three women aged 35 and older said they were unsure whether they were in perimenopause. Many did not know whether their symptoms reflected hormonal changes, especially if their periods remained regular and/or they had other health conditions. About 16% attributed their uncertainty to barriers such as dismissive healthcare encounters, including being told they were “too young.” 2

Uncertainty was highest among women with the most severe symptoms. Put another way, the women who might benefit most from intervention were the least likely to know they were in perimenopause.

Adding to the problem, some women experience perimenopause symptoms while their menstrual cycles remain regular. They may not develop obvious menstrual changes until later in the transition or until they’ve entered menopause.

“It is a universal transition with ambiguity and often lack of validation,” the study’s authors wrote. Better symptom recognition and communication are critical first steps toward improving care.

[Free Self-Test: Is It Perimenopause?]

Keep a Perimenopause Log

Use the Perimenopause Log below to identify patterns and track physical symptoms and symptoms related to mood, menstrual cycle, ADHD, cognition, and ADHD medication efficacy. Keep a journal to record more specific experiences, such as missing an appointment, losing words during a conversation, or forgetting to complete tasks that once seemed manageable.

Perimenopause Log

Symptoms 0 = none 1 = minimal 2 = moderate 3 = high

Constipation

Fatigue

Breast Tenderness

Vaginal Dryness

Pain During Sex

Sadness or low mood

Irritability

Anger or rage

Overwhelm

Less interest in sex

Brain Fog

Mucus secretions

Increased/Decreased flow or bleeding

Difficulty focusing

Difficulty planning or organizing

Forgetfulness

Impulsivity

Degraded efficacy

Shorter duration of coverage

Elevated side effects

Hormone Testing for Perimenopause

Hormone tests are typically used when symptoms begin earlier than expected, the diagnosis is unclear, or another condition is suspected. If testing is warranted, your doctor should look at follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) and estrogen levels. FSH signals the ovaries to develop eggs and produce estradiol. As ovarian function becomes less predictable during perimenopause, FSH generally rises to compensate while estradiol fluctuates. Multiple tests may be needed to establish a clear picture.

If you are younger than 40 and have irregular periods along with perimenopause symptoms, ask whether you should be evaluated for primary ovarian insufficiency (POI), a condition that requires careful treatment.

Finding Stability in Perimenopause

I use the term “hormone stabilization therapy,” or HST, to describe hormonal treatment during perimenopause, when the goal is to smooth disruptive hormonal swings rather than replace hormones after menstruation has ended.

Certainty that you are in perimenopause is not required before discussing relief. Treatment should focus on the symptoms causing the greatest problems even while your doctor continues to evaluate their source.

Medication Considerations in Perimenopause

Estrogen therapy relieves symptoms of perimenopause and may improve the effectiveness of ADHD stimulant medication. Prior use of estrogen therapy may render it less effective. If you have a uterus and use systemic estrogen, you will need a progestogen to protect the uterine lining. The type and schedule should be individualized. Vaginal estrogen may be an option for urinary or vaginal symptoms, including for some women who cannot use systemic treatment.

ADHD medication may need to change — whether it’s the dose, timing, or type of medication — during this stage for continued symptom control.

Birth control is often recommended for its role in smoothing hormonal fluctuations. In my clinical experience, progestin-only birth control may worsen mood and symptoms of ADHD in some women Talk to your provider about your symptoms and whether other birth control options may be right for you.

Antidepressants and other non-hormonal medications can help manage hot flashes, mood swings, anxiety, and depression.

Keep track of your results. When possible, make one planned change at a time so it will be easier to understand what has helped.

Women with ADHD need support during perimenopause. Take your needs seriously and seek counseling, ADHD coaching, or peer-support groups to help you find new ways to cope.

Perimenopausal Symptoms: Next Steps

Do you have a fear of leaving the house? How to cope with agoraphobia

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

Living with agoraphobia can feel isolating. Discover 9 clinically backed ways to ease your fear of leaving the house and start taking confident steps toward calm and freedom.

Some days, even the thought of stepping outside can make your chest tighten. The world feels too big, too unpredictable, and your home feels like the only place that's safe. This experience is more common than most people realize, and it often points to something called agoraphobia, or the fear of leaving the house.

People who experience agoraphobia aren't necessarily weak, but rather trying to create safety and control in their day-to-day experience. This particular phobia can stem from intense experiences like trauma, but can also be rooted in genetics and lived experience. While there's nothing wrong with wanting to feel safe at home, agoraphobia can make everyday tasks like running errands or going to work feel daunting and overwhelming.

Learning to ease back into exploring the world at large can help make life feel more… well, livable. So, let's explore what causes agoraphobia, what can make it worse, and a few evidence-based ways to start rebuilding a sense of ease in the world and the return of your freedom.

 

What causes agoraphobia (or the fear of leaving the house)?

Agoraphobia often starts with a negative experience. A panic attack on the subway, a dizzy spell in a grocery store, a sudden wave of fear that seems to come from nowhere. When that happens, the body remembers and starts building invisible boundaries to prevent it from happening again. What starts as caution can slowly turn into avoidance, until even ordinary outings feel overwhelming.

Clinically, agoraphobia is often linked to anxiety or panic disorders, though it can develop on its own. The fear isn't about the world itself, but about losing control or facing uncomfortable sensations in public. If you've ever thought, “What if I panic and can't escape?” that's the loop agoraphobia reinforces. And your brain's attempt to protect you ends up feeding the fear.

Several factors can play a role:

  • Past panic attacks or trauma: Experiences that felt unsafe or uncontrollable can leave the body on high alert long after the danger has passed.

  • Chronic stress or burnout: When your nervous system is constantly under strain, it becomes more reactive to triggers.

  • Family history or genetics: Anxiety disorders can run in families, likely due to a mix of inherited traits and learned coping patterns.

  • Personality and temperament: People who tend to be more sensitive, cautious, or introspective may notice and internalize physical sensations more deeply, which can heighten fear responses.

  • Other mental health conditions: Depression, social anxiety, and PTSD can all overlap with or contribute to agoraphobic symptoms.

Read more: What are the most common phobias? Plus, how to treat them

 

What are the symptoms of agoraphobia?

Agoraphobia doesn't always look the same for everyone. For some, it's a strong hesitation to go far from home. For others, it means avoiding specific situations where escape might feel difficult or embarrassing, like crowded stores, public transit, or open spaces. The fear isn't necessarily about the place itself, but about what might happen once you're there.

These symptoms can range from mild to severe and may come and go. You might still leave the house sometimes, but only under specific conditions, or you might find that even opening the door feels unbearable. Here are a few common symptoms:

Physical symptoms often overlap with panic or anxiety responses. You might notice:

  • A racing heartbeat, shortness of breath, or dizziness

  • Sweating, trembling, or nausea

  • Feeling detached from your surroundings or like you might faint

  • Chest tightness or a sense that something terrible is about to happen

Emotional and behavioral symptoms can develop as you try to avoid those sensations:

  • Avoiding certain places or activities

  • Relying on a friend or family member to accompany you outside

  • Feeling trapped, frustrated, or ashamed for not being able to “just go out”

  • Increased anxiety or depression from isolation

What can trigger agoraphobia or make it worse?

Triggers can vary widely, but they usually share one thing: they stir feelings of uncertainty or vulnerability. A crowded concert, a long commute, a sudden noise, or even imagining an unfamiliar situation can spark a fear response.

Common triggers include:

  • Crowded or enclosed spaces, like buses, malls, or elevators

  • Open or exposed places, such as parking lots or wide streets

  • Traveling alone, even short distances

  • Previous panic experiences, especially if they happened in public

  • Health anxiety, such as fearing a medical emergency far from home

Avoidance often feels like relief in the short term, but over time, it reinforces the fear. The brain learns that staying home equals safety, while leaving equals danger.

Agoraphobia can also worsen during periods of high stress or change. Even positive life events—like moving, starting a new job, or entering a relationship—can unsettle a nervous system already wired for hypervigilance.

Recovery focuses on gently retraining your body to tolerate fear without letting it dictate your choices. It's a gradual, compassionate process that rebuilds trust in the world beyond your walls.

Related read: What it actually means to “be triggered” (and how to cope)

 

How to cope with the fear of going outside: 9 strategies for dealing with agoraphobia

When you're living with agoraphobia, recovery can be tricky. You don't want to force yourself to go outside if you don't feel safe. The idea is to build a sense of safety first, starting small and working up to leaving the house. These strategies are drawn from clinical research and real-world practice and are designed to help you move at your own pace while gently retraining your brain and body to trust the world again.

1. Start with understanding, not avoidance

It’s important to remember that avoiding what scares you isn’t a sign of weakness; it’s your brain trying to protect you. Your nervous system probably learned that certain places or situations feel unsafe, so it tries to keep you away. 

Agoraphobia sets off a real physiological fear response. When the brain senses threat, it switches on the nervous system's fight-or-flight mode, so your heart rate climbs, your breathing goes shallow, and your muscles tense. Knowing this takes some of the power out of it. You're not going crazy. Your brain is doing its job, in the wrong context.

Start by observing your fear with curiosity. Ask yourself, when does it appear? What sensations come first, what thoughts follow? This kind of mindful attention helps you separate real danger from false alarms. A short daily journal—just a few lines—can make it easier to spot patterns.

💙 If anxiety or panic catches you off guard, reach for Tamara Levitt's Panic SOS session on Calm to work with your breath and body when you need it most.

2. Practice grounding techniques when fear hits

When panic or anxiety rises, grounding practices can help your body remember it’s safe right now. These techniques calm the stress response by reconnecting you with your senses.

Try the 5–4–3–2–1 method:

  • 5 things you can see

  • 4 you can hear

  • 3 you can feel

  • 2 you can smell

  • 1 you can taste

If going outside feels overwhelming, start at home — by an open window or your doorway. Gradually, your body learns that calm is possible beyond your comfort zone.

💙 Try the 5-4-3-2-1 grounding technique on the Calm app.

3. Gradual exposure, not all-at-once challenges

Gradual exposure therapy—facing feared situations in small, structured steps—is one of the most evidence-based ways to ease agoraphobia. Start where your anxiety feels manageable, then increase the challenge as confidence grows.

Try this:

  1. Sit near your front door for a few minutes.

  2. Step outside and take a few breaths.

  3. Walk to the end of your street.

  4. Visit a quiet store or café during off-hours.

The aim isn't to feel no anxiety. It's to stay in the situation long enough for anxiety to come down on its own, and to show yourself, again and again, that you can handle it. Each step helps your brain link “outside” with safety. Celebrate even the smallest wins. Progress may be uneven, but every step counts.

💙 Explore Ease Your Social Anxiety with Jeff Warren on Calm, designed to help your world expand again at your own pace.

4. Challenge avoidance patterns

Avoidance is the mechanism that keeps agoraphobia in place. Every time anxiety pushes you to cancel plans or stay home, and you give in, it reinforces the idea that the feared situation was dangerous. Over time, the list of “unsafe” places and situations tends to grow.

Noticing when avoidance is happening, and gently questioning it, is part of recovery. That doesn't mean forcing yourself into overwhelming situations. It means asking: is this decision based on actual risk, or on anxiety talking?

💙 Turn toward what you're avoiding rather than away from it with Emotions Series: Investigating Anxiety, led by John Armstrong on Calm.

5. Work with a therapist who understands anxiety disorders

A licensed therapist, especially one trained in cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) or exposure therapy, can help you challenge anxious thoughts and gradually expand your comfort zone.

If therapy feels intimidating, try starting with a telehealth session. Many people with agoraphobia begin remotely and move to in-person care later. Therapists can also teach tools like cognitive restructuring (questioning catastrophic thoughts) and interoceptive exposure (reducing fear of physical sensations).

If cost is a barrier, ask about low-cost or sliding-scale sessions.

Read more: How CBT can help calm your anxiety (and 5 ways to try it today)

 

6. Strengthen your support network

Agoraphobia can feel isolating, but recovery is rarely something you do alone. Support from trusted friends, family, or peer groups can help you stay connected when your world feels small.

Identify one or two people who can listen without minimizing your fear. Be clear about what helps, like “Please don't push me to go out, but check in after I try.”

Online support communities for agoraphobia or panic disorder can also be powerful spaces to share progress and encouragement. Connection doesn't have to mean going far, just not going alone.

Related read: 7 tips on how to communicate your needs in a relationship

7. Regulate your body before you face your fears

Your nervous system can't process new experiences if it's stuck in survival mode. Before trying exposure work, focus on calming your baseline stress.

Try this:

  • Slow breathing (inhale 4 counts, exhale 6)

  • Gentle stretching or yoga

  • A consistent sleep routine

  • Regular meals to prevent low blood sugar

  • Limiting caffeine or alcohol if they spike anxiety

The longer exhale matters. It activates the parasympathetic nervous system, which helps the body downshift from a high-alert state. These habits won't "cure" agoraphobia, but they can give your body the steadiness it needs to face fear safely.

8. Use mindfulness to build tolerance for discomfort

Mindfulness doesn't mean clearing your mind, but noticing thoughts and sensations without reacting to them. When you can observe fear rather than fight it, it starts to lose its grip.

Next time panic rises, name it: 

“This is fear”. 

Then focus on your breath or the feeling of your feet on the floor. You're not trying to make the fear vanish, just reminding yourself you can stay present while it passes. With practice, your brain can learn that fear doesn't always mean danger.

💙 Explore these Tools for Anxiety and Stress with Dr. Julie on the Calm app.

9. Take recovery one small step at a time

Progress with agoraphobia often feels like two steps forward, one step back. Some days you'll walk outside easily; others, just getting dressed takes effort. That's okay.

Set small, specific goals like opening a window, walking to the mailbox, or standing outside for 30 seconds. Track and celebrate each one. Speak to yourself as you would to a friend since compassion keeps recovery moving when motivation fades.

There will be days when something that felt manageable last week feels impossible again. That's not failure. It's a normal feature of recovering from an anxiety disorder. What matters is returning to your plan after a setback rather than writing off the progress you've made.

 

Can mindfulness help with agoraphobia?

Mindfulness won't cure agoraphobia, but it can help you manage it. There's research that suggests mindfulness may reduce anxiety by helping people look at fearful thoughts and body sensations without getting swept up in them.

That matters with agoraphobia. Fear isn't just about the situation you're in. It also sets off a chain of anxious thoughts that make physical symptoms feel worse. Mindfulness helps you notice that chain without automatically following it.

Even five to ten minutes of meditation a day may help change how your brain responds to fear over time. It won't make anxiety go away, but it can make it easier to handle. For anyone who finds leaving home hard, a home-based app like Calm makes this practice easy to access without any extra pressure.

Read more: Mindfulness for beginners: 6 techniques for mindful living

When should you seek professional help for agoraphobia?

Agoraphobia is treatable. With the right combination of therapy, support, and sometimes medication, many people reclaim a sense of calm and freedom they thought was lost.

Reaching out for help when you can barely leave the house can feel impossible. For many people with agoraphobia, even calling a therapist can trigger anxiety. But asking for help is simply a sign you're ready for support.

Consider professional help if your fear limits work, school, or relationships; if you feel trapped at the thought of leaving home; or if you depend on others to go out. When your world starts shrinking, or hopelessness sets in, it's time for extra care. Early treatment also tends to be more effective than waiting for symptoms to worsen, and it doesn't require hitting a low point first.

What are the most common phobias?

Curious to learn more about other phobias and how to treat them?  Having a deeper understanding of common phobias, like acrophobia or metathesiophobia, can help you get to know yourself better and give you tools for treating them or easing their symptoms.
