Fear of leaving the house FAQs

Why do I have a fear of leaving my house?

The fear of leaving the house often develops after experiences that made you feel unsafe or out of control — like a panic attack in public, trauma, or chronic stress. Over time, your brain links "outside" with danger and "home" with safety.

What are the signs that I have agoraphobia?

Common signs include anxiety or panic outside the home, fear of crowds, public transport, or open and enclosed spaces, and a strong urge to avoid those situations. Some people can only manage them with someone else present. Physical symptoms can include a racing heart, dizziness, shortness of breath, and chest tightness. If this is limiting where you go or what you do, talk to a doctor or mental health professional.

Is fear of leaving the house the same as agoraphobia?

Not always, but they often overlap. Agoraphobia is a clinical term for anxiety about situations where escape might be difficult or help unavailable during panic. The fear of leaving home can be one part of that pattern. Some people feel anxious in only a few situations, while others avoid most public places.

Is agoraphobia the same as social anxiety?

No, though they can overlap. Social anxiety is about fear of judgment from others. Agoraphobia is about fear of situations where escape feels difficult or panic might happen. Both involve avoidance, but the triggers are different and so are the treatment approaches.

What can trigger agoraphobia?

Common triggers include crowded or open spaces, traveling alone, driving, public transit, or past experiences of panic. Emotional stress, health worries, or major life changes can also heighten symptoms. 

Triggers are deeply personal; learning what sets off your fear is a key part of recovery, because it helps you prepare, plan, and respond with more calm.

Can you be agoraphobic and still leave the house?

Yes. Many people with agoraphobia still leave home but limit when, where, or how they do it — maybe only with a trusted companion or in familiar areas. Agoraphobia exists on a spectrum, and partial avoidance is common. 

The goal of treatment isn’t to eliminate all fear, but to help you feel more confident and capable outside, even when anxiety shows up.

How can I slowly start leaving the house again?

Start with very small steps and build gradually. Open the door and stand outside for a minute. Walk to the mailbox. Sit in your car. Over time, extend your distance or duration. Pair these moments with grounding techniques and self-compassion.

When should I see a therapist about agoraphobia?

If fear or avoidance is getting in the way of work, relationships, or daily life, it's a good time to reach out. You don't need to be housebound to ask for help. A therapist who specializes in anxiety can offer structured treatment, including CBT and exposure work, that's hard to replicate on your own.

How do I deal with agoraphobia?

Agoraphobia recovery combines understanding, gradual exposure, nervous system regulation, and professional support. Grounding techniques, mindfulness, and therapy—especially cognitive behavioral or exposure therapy—can help retrain your fear response. 

Compassion is essential; you’re learning to reintroduce safety to your body, not forcing yourself to be “brave.”

How long does it take to overcome agoraphobia?

It varies. Some people see real improvement within a few months of consistent CBT and exposure work. Others take a year or more. Progress is usually gradual and setbacks are normal. How long it takes depends on severity, how long symptoms have been present, and access to treatment. Consistency matters more than speed.

Is exposure therapy safe for agoraphobia?

Yes, when done properly. Good exposure therapy is gradual. It starts with situations that bring up mild anxiety and builds slowly from there. It should never mean being pushed into something overwhelming without preparation. Working with a trained therapist keeps the pace right and makes sure coping tools are in place before each step.

What are the best treatment options for fear of leaving home?

The most effective treatments are cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), exposure therapy, and sometimes medication like SSRIs or SNRIs. Teletherapy can also help if in-person sessions feel too difficult. 

Many people benefit from combining approaches that balance science with emotional safety.

Can meditation really help with agoraphobia?

Yes, when used gently. Meditation can help calm the body’s stress response and increase awareness of anxious thoughts without getting caught in them. 

However, for some people, sitting still with intense anxiety can feel overwhelming. Start small—guided breathing, body scans, or walking meditations—and use it as one tool among others, not a cure-all.

What are the benefits of overcoming agoraphobia?

Recovery tends to reopen parts of life that anxiety had closed off: work, relationships, travel, and everyday spontaneity. Many people also notice a broader shift in confidence, and find they start attempting things they'd written off, not just outside the home, but in other areas where anxiety had been calling the shots.

Can agoraphobia go away on its own?

Symptoms can lessen over time, especially with self-awareness and stress reduction, but full recovery usually requires intentional support. Without treatment, the avoidance cycle can deepen. 

The good news is that with therapy and consistent practice, many people regain independence and confidence, even after years of fear.

Calm your mind. Change your life.

New Perinatal Guidance for Women with ADHD

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

During pregnancy and the post-partum period, hormonal fluctuations and mounting demands on executive function intensify ADHD symptoms. Poor memory, dysregulated emotions, and disrupted sleep become elevated (and exhausting) during an already-stressful time.

Paradoxically, the vast majority of pregnant women with ADHD stop treating the condition with medication on the advice of their healthcare providers. Patients have been historically told to cease using stimulant and non-stimulant medication during pregnancy due to a lack of safety data and fear of potential risks to the fetus. But new research has produced generally reassuring reproductive safety data — and pushed more providers to weigh risks on both sides of the treatment equation.

How might untreated ADHD impact a woman’s mental health and her ability to manage the extraordinary demands of caring for an infant? Could ceasing treatment pose a bigger risk to the fetus than continuing it? What are the dangers associated with abruptly stopping treatment without monitoring, support, or a plan? These questions deserve to be asked.

Not every woman should continue medication, but neither should every woman stop. This decision should account for symptom severity, individual history, co-occurring conditions, gestational risks, and the consequences of untreated ADHD. These factors should be discussed with a medical team over multiple visits.

Use these recommendations — adapted in part from guidelines published in the American Journal of Obstetrics & Gynecology — to begin conversations with your medical team about your health and your baby’s health.

[Read: We Demand Attention on the Safety and Efficacy of ADHD Medication Use During Pregnancy]

Preconception

If you take a stimulant, talk with your prescriber about whether a supervised trial without medication is appropriate. Discontinuation may be reasonable for some women with mild ADHD who function well with non-pharmacological supports.

Pay attention to changes in:

  • Work and household functioning
  • Driving and other safety-related activities
  • Sleep, nutrition, and self-care
  • Mood and emotional regulation
  • Relationships and overall quality of life

[Read: ADHD Meds Should Not Be “Panic Stopped” in Pregnancy]

It is reasonable to continue stimulant treatment if it is required for daily functioning. Discuss these options with your doctor:

  • Continuing your current medication
  • Using the lowest effective dose
  • Taking medication intermittently
  • Switching to a non-stimulant, if appropriate

Pregnancy

Most studies have shown no adverse effects among infants exposed to maternal ADHD medications. Amphetamines may increase the risk of preeclampsia and other adverse gestational outcomes, but the absolute risk appears low. Meanwhile, studies show that discontinuing medication during pregnancy may worsen ADHD symptoms, mental health, and daily functioning.

Weigh All Risks

Medication decisions should consider the risks associated with a fetus’ medication exposure and those associated with untreated ADHD. Some women choose to limit or stop medication during pregnancy and optimize treatment after delivery. If stimulant use is a concern, ask about lowering the dose, using medication intermittently, or considering another treatment.

Relevant factors include:

  • Trimester of pregnancy
  • ADHD symptom severity and functional impairment
  • Co-occurring physical and mental health conditions
  • Gestational hypertension, preeclampsia, and other vascular or placental risks

Attend Prenatal Appointments

Regular care allows your clinician to monitor fetal growth as well as your blood pressure, weight, and other health measures that may be affected by stimulant use.

Establish Support

Work with your care team to anticipate changes in symptoms, sleep, and mood in the postpartum period — a sensitive time when you may need greater support. Your proactive plan might include:

  • Therapy or ADHD coaching
  • Prenatal and postpartum doula support
  • Midwifery or obstetric follow-up
  • Pre-scheduled postpartum and psychiatric appointments
  • A plan for meals, chores, transportation, and overnight care
  • Clear responsibilities for partners and family members
  • A list of warning signs and whom to contact for help

Postpartum

Research has found that:

  • Methylphenidate exposure through milk is very low and generally undetected in infants.
  • Amphetamines transfer into milk at somewhat higher levels, though overall infant risk appears low.
  • Studies on non-stimulants and lactation are few.

Ask your provider:

  • If you take an amphetamine derivative and have milder symptoms, could intermittent use or coordinating medication and feeding schedules reduce infant exposure? Depending on your needs and preferences, should combination feeding or formula be considered?
  • If your pre-pregnancy dose no longer provides adequate symptom control after delivery, ask about the risks and benefits of adjusting treatment to support mood, functioning, and safety for both you and your baby.

Postpartum Support

Inattention, emotional dysregulation, and executive dysfunction intensify in the postpartum period. Women with ADHD face a substantially greater risk of postpartum mood and anxiety symptoms.

  • Be candid with your providers about changes in mood, anxiety, sleep, functioning, or your ability to care for yourself and your baby.
  • Consider therapy, ADHD coaching, postpartum support groups, midwifery care, and/or a postpartum doula. A doula can provide infant care education, feeding support, help with household routines, and opportunities for rest. A midwife may help with clinical postpartum care, assess recovery, support feeding, and identify concerns requiring additional treatment.
  • Reduce executive-function demands by:
    • Automating grocery and household deliveries
    • Using shared calendars and written checklists
    • Limiting commitments

Can I Take ADHD Meds While Pregnant? Next Steps

Can You Learn to Feel More Loved?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Imagine you have a colleague who remembers that you had a difficult appointment and checks in on you. Or a friend who is just there to listen without telling you what you should have done differently. We don’t tend to think of these experiences as grand declarations of love, but they leave us with a certain lingering warmth: the feeling of being loved.

In our research, we coined the term felt love to focus on the receiver’s side of love. This emphasis shifts attention away from the expressions of love (i.e., loving signals). While expressions are actions we choose to take, felt love is an internal, subjective sensation that washes over us. However, how strongly it does so also depends on us: The same act of care may pass unnoticed or land as love, based on the receiver’s cognitive beliefs or state of mind. Many experiences may elicit felt love, and they occur not only in romantic settings, but when we share time with friends, receive supportive gestures from family, or even get unexpected help from strangers. We are only just beginning to uncover how people come to feel loved and whether that experience can be changed—if it can be learned, like a skill.

In our research on shared beliefs about love, we asked 495 people representative of the U.S. population to judge situations that would make most people feel loved. What they ranked most love-eliciting was being shown compassion in difficult times—an act that, in principle, any person could (and should) offer. Pets feeling happy to see them ranked highly for eliciting the feeling of being loved. Clearly, love, as people live it day to day, reaches past courtship and partnership.

Work like this partly drives how we measure who feels loved and when. It is common for studies to measure the experience of love as if it were a stable trait—for example, romantic attachment as a demographic indicator—but this practice obscures fundamental aspects of the lived human story. Relationship status may describe part of someone’s circumstances, but it does not tell us whether that person feels loved in their daily experience. For example, many people experience love while unattached, and many do not experience love despite being in a long-term relationship. In fact, in our studies, strong experiences of felt love are reported by participants who identify as unattached. To study felt love, we also have to ask about the experience directly.

Tracking felt love in real time in real life

Subjective states like felt love are inherently volatile, so studying it requires many careful observations, ideally while people are going about their daily lives. We often rely on complex research methods that focus not only on how people feel at a given time, but on the patterns of change over time and how those are shaped by our environment and affected by others.
 
Our basic approach is simple in principle: Ask people how loved they feel at different times while they go about their lives. In two studies published in 2020, in which over 200 participants reported their felt love states a handful of times per day for several weeks, we found that people with higher levels of felt love also tended to report greater flourishing and emotional well-being. 

Frequent, repeated observations also allow us to disentangle a person’s typical experience from day-to-day fluctuations. This can also reveal individual differences in how persistent daily felt love states are—a feeling can be strong and brief, or weaker but sustained. Persistence is not automatically desirable: A below-usual feeling of being loved can persist, too. Whether particular patterns directly affect well-being remains an important open question.

To study the role of persistence, we developed custom mathematical models that include love inertia—that is, how long that warm feeling stays with us. In our recently published study, 52 participants reported how loved they felt as well as how much love they had expressed over a period of 28 days. We found that people whose felt love states were more persistent also enjoyed better mental health and indicators of well-being. Even more interestingly, the more people expressed love to others in their daily lives, the more they themselves tended to feel loved in return. Expressing love appears to be a gift that keeps on giving: Showing love is associated with a lasting sense of warmth and better well-being, and it also spreads love around you.

This finding suggests an actionable insight: Expressing love could be one route to feeling loved. If you are feeling a lack of connection, proactively expressing selfless care for someone else might help jumpstart your own feelings of being loved. We don’t yet know what accounts for this association. Expressing love might invite a warm response, change how you interpret a relationship, or be part of an interaction that lifts both people up.

Can we learn to feel more loved?

Can an interaction actually change how loved someone feels? A 2024 study with 218 parent–child pairs argues just that. The authors of that study randomly assigned parents and their children, aged eight to 13, to one of two nine-minute conversations. In one condition, they took turns answering questions that encouraged personal disclosure (for example, instead of a surface-level question like “How was school?,” they might ask, “What is something you worry about?”). In the other condition, they made small talk. Children in the disclosure condition reported feeling more loved during the conversation.

This result cannot readily be explained by children in warmer families simply choosing more personal conversations—assignment of children to conditions was random. The researchers changed the conversation and observed a direct difference in the children’s experience.

Such findings need to be independently replicated for reliability and generalizability, but this is an important and promising step: A straightforward intervention changed an experience of feeling loved. The next question we can work on is whether changes of this kind can become part of daily life.

Our focus on the receiver side of love brings a slightly more complex perspective: that an act does not produce a loving feeling by itself, but that effect is moderated by interpretation and expectation—thoughts that come to us in the moment, cognitive beliefs that support those thoughts, and the justifications we have for those beliefs. A colleague’s quiet help can be experienced as love, or dismissed as routine professional courtesy.

An area that is not yet well-explored is how the reception of expressions of love can be taught. If feelings follow perceptions, and perceptions follow beliefs, then felt love is a cognitive phenomenon in addition to an emotional one, so that cognitive skills contribute to how we experience the social world. It may be possible for people to learn to notice when they reflexively dismiss acts of care, to check their interpretation against what actually happened, and to re-evaluate in order to let the sender’s kindness count. It is also possible that a deficit in this cognitive skill prohibits many people from benefiting from the ambient love that is already present in everyday life. Even so, biased perceptions can have positive effects in this context. People with a stronger general tendency to interpret daily life experiences as expressions of love feel more loved in general, with all the well-being benefits that brings.

Where the science goes from here

We now have ways to measure felt love in daily life, evidence linking it to well-being, and at least one experiment that changed it in the moment. A sensible next step is to connect those findings.

Can an intervention change daily feelings of love for weeks or months? Does that change lead to better well-being? Does it work for people who start out feeling relatively unloved, or mainly for those already doing well? Those are different questions, all embedded in the vastly complex network of our daily environments and experiences—part of what is sometimes called the exposome—and they require different, carefully drawn comparisons.

Future trials could focus on the cognitive component of felt love—whether an underlying ability to appreciate acts of love extends to other facets of life, and whether interventions should target the individual’s reasoning before focusing on their social context. Repeated measurements in daily-life settings could be used to show whether any differences generated by the intervention survive after the study session. Such trials would need to be designed with a great deal of care to establish the mechanisms of causality—a standard challenge of daily-life social psychology studies, but an especially critical one here. Establishing how to make experiences of felt love more available is an expansive but worthwhile scientific endeavor.

Finally, an emerging focus on love also recognizes how it is culturally and contextually bound. While certain loving gestures are found all over the world (such as helping someone in need, or empathic attention), to foster intercultural understanding, empathy, and inclusivity in diverse societies, it is vital to recognize love’s cultural variations in everyday life. We are currently conducting global cross-cultural research to identify specific ways love is shown in everyday life across different types of relationships, beyond just the romantic.

Do try this at home

While we and other researchers continue to conduct larger studies to unpack these exact mechanisms, we don’t have to wait for the data to start paying closer attention to the quiet moments in our own lives. What happens when you feel genuinely cared for? What happens when you proactively express love to someone else? Being mindful of how they respond can reveal something that checking a box to count affectionate gestures misses. Fostering these experiences doesn’t require a laboratory—it is a daily practice we can all begin right now. Showing love is a low-cost experiment we can all conduct in our own daily lives.

Where is the best place to meditate? Here’s how to find your spot

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

Psst: there's no “best” place to meditate. Some spots just make it feel more natural. Here's how to find the right location for your next meditation session, indoors or out.

You have a free morning and want to meditate, but you’re not sure where. The living room isn’t private or quiet enough. The lighting in your home office is harsh. In your bedroom, the only place to sit is your bed, and you’re worried you might fall asleep. So instead of practicing mindfulness, you end up weighing the risks of distraction or discomfort.

The good news is, you don't need a Pinterest-worthy space, just a spot that works for you. People practice on train platforms, in parked cars, and on office floors during their lunch break, and they still get the benefits.

Whether you're just getting started or you’ve been meditating for years, this guide will help you find and level up that spot. 

 

What makes a good meditation space?

A good meditation space is one where you feel safe, comfortable, and undisturbed. These four qualities tend to make the biggest difference:

  • Comfort: A supportive cushion, a chair with a good backrest, or even a spot against the wall reduces strain on your body. 

  • Quiet: Minimal noise, foot traffic, or notifications helps you avoid competition for your attention.

  • Calming sensory cues: Soft lamplight, a candle, a diffuser, or a warm blanket can signal it’s time to slow down.

  • Familiarity: The same corner or cushion becomes a cue over time, so your brain starts to wind down as soon as you arrive.

💙 Just starting out? Here’s a detailed guide on how to meditate for beginners.

Where is the best place to meditate?

The best place to meditate depends on what you want from your session, and many people rotate between a few spots. Indoors is more predictable, since weather and temperature are rarely a factor. Outdoors, on the other hand, can feel more grounding and restorative.

Indoor meditation spots

When you meditate indoors, you have more control over the temperature, lighting, and setup of the space, which can help you build a consistent habit. 

Your bedroom corner. It's private and may already feel like a place to recharge, so it works well when you want to slow down. 

Your living room. This spot works best when it’s empty, like early morning before anyone's up or once the house has gone quiet for the night.

Your unused nook or closet. With just a cushion and a curtain, almost any small, overlooked area can become your own little sanctuary.

Your bed. Meditating here is a gentle way to start your day or transition into sleep, though the same comfort that makes it appealing can also make you doze off before you're done.

💙 For ways to turn any of these spots into a dedicated space, see Calm's meditation room ideas. 

💙 For more on the bed option specifically, check out bedtime meditation techniques.

 

Outdoor meditation spots

The outdoors offers something special: fresh air, the open sky, and nature sounds that help calm your nervous system. Time in green space is linked to lower cortisol and reduced stress. One explanation is the attention restoration theory — the idea that natural settings give your focus a chance to recover.

A backyard or garden. If you have one, some greenery and a comfortable seat can turn it into a peaceful retreat.

A beach or park. Either works especially well near water, where the sound of waves or a stream can ease your mind. 

A balcony or patch of grass. Even a small outdoor space gives you a change of scenery, which can help you relax.

💙 For more on practicing outside, see Calm’s guides on outdoor meditation and building a meditation garden.

If sitting feels hard, try walking meditation. Focus on the rhythm of your steps to give your mind something to settle on.

💙 Here’s how to start walking mindfully.  

How to optimize your meditation space: 5 tips 

A few small, deliberate changes can turn your meditation space into one that genuinely supports your practice.

1. Set up a regular spot 

Store your cushion, blanket, and anything else you use to meditate in your dedicated place. When you return to the same spot consistently, ideally around the same time, settling in can start to feel automatic. 

2. Create a little privacy

Face away from clutter, toward a blank wall or window. In a shared space, a folding screen or blanket draped over a chair can mark off your corner. Even with your eyes closed, feeling tucked away can help you settle.

3. Choose the right sound 

Try playing steady background noise—like soothing nature sounds or music—to cover unpredictable and intrusive sounds like traffic or a neighbor's TV. If you prefer some structure in your practice, you could follow a guided meditation. 

To slow down, experiment with tracks around 60–80 beats per minute, which researchers have linked to a slower heart rate. For daytime focus, many people find that ambient or instrumental sound works best. 

💙 Calm's guides on meditation music can help you find what fits your needs. 

4. Minimize distractions 

Put your phone on Do Not Disturb, and tell the people you live with that you'll be unavailable for the length of your session. You could also pick a time when they’re less likely to need you. 

💙 Try the Single-Tasking session with Jay Shetty on Calm. 

5. Use props for good posture

If you need to take the strain off your lower back, sit on a cushion or folded blanket to raise your hips slightly above your knees. You can also meditate near a wall, or place a chair nearby to support your spine if sitting upright gets tiring. 

A meditation cushion can help you hold your position more comfortably. Try keeping the room on the cool side too, since an overly warm setup can nudge you toward sleep.

💙 Explore the Posture session from the 7 Days of Calm series. 

💙 Learn about the different meditation postures to find the right fit for you.

 

Where is the best place to meditate? FAQs

Does where I meditate really matter?

Where you meditate shapes how the practice feels once you're there, but consistency is what matters most. A pleasant space can motivate you to start, though it's not required.

What if I don't have a quiet place to meditate?

You can still meditate in a noisy home or busy neighborhood, and plenty of people do. Headphones with a guided session or ambient sound can add a bit of calm almost anywhere, even a parked car or a bathroom. 

You can even let the noise become part of the practice by resting your attention on the sounds themselves, instead of trying to push them away. Over time, that flexibility often makes your practice stronger.

How do I set up a meditation corner in a small space?

You don’t need much to set up a meditation corner. A cushion, a clear patch of floor, and some calming objects are enough. Pick an empty corner and mark it off with a plant, a small rug, or a curtain. 

Can I meditate in bed?

Meditating in bed is fine, and it's a gentle way to wind down at night or start your morning. If you’re worried about dozing off, sit up against the headboard instead of lying down, or try it earlier in the day when you’re more alert.

Is it better to meditate indoors or outdoors?

The better choice depends on what you need that day. Indoors gives you privacy and control, which suits a daily habit, while outdoors adds fresh air and a grounding connection to nature. Plenty of people mix the two.

How do I reduce distractions when meditating?

To reduce distractions when you meditate, start with what you can control: silence your phone and pick a time when interruptions are less likely.

For noises that still come up, treat your breath as your anchor and return to it whenever your mind wanders. 

💙 Calm's guides on what to focus on during meditation and how to improve focus go deeper on taming a wandering mind.

What should a meditation space look like?

Your meditation space doesn't need to look a certain way. It just needs to work for you. Soft light, minimal clutter, and something comfortable to sit on are usually enough. 

Can music or sounds help me meditate?

Music and calming sounds can ease you into your meditation. Soft instrumental tracks, nature sounds, or steady background noise all help mask distracting noises and signal to your body that it’s time to relax. 

As your practice develops, you might find you prefer silence, so it's worth experimenting to see what suits you.

💙 Learn how to meditate with music. 

Calm your mind. Change your life.

Mental health is hard. Getting support doesn't have to be. The Calm app puts the tools to feel better in your back pocket, with personalized content to manage stress and anxiety, get better sleep, and feel more present in your life.

 

Early Puberty More Common in Girls with ADHD

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Puberty is not a single event, but a years-long process that is both universal and highly individual. It shapes brain and emotional development, identity, and lifelong physical and mental health. If it begins before a child is developmentally ready, it can increase the risk for physical and mental health problems.

Research suggests that girls with ADHD are more likely to enter puberty early and to experience the transition more intensely, placing them at heightened risk for anxiety, depression, eating disorders, and other challenges. Parental guidance, emotional regulation skills, and professional support can buffer these risks by bolstering self-confidence in girls as they navigate the pubertal transition.

The Early Onset of Puberty

Researchers are examining why girls with ADHD have about twice the risk for early puberty, and why autistic girls are up to four times more likely to experience early puberty compared to other children.1 In addition to higher rates of obesity and social stress, which could accelerate the onset of puberty, neurodivergent girls may experience differences in dopamine activity, stress response, and immune and neuroendocrine systems — all of which may affect sexual development.

What Is Precocious Puberty?

Early relative puberty — development that begins earlier than average but remains within the typical age range — is different from precocious puberty. In girls, precocious puberty refers to physical and hormonal changes before age 8, including breast and pubic hair development, rapid growth, and menstruation. It requires medical evaluation and treatment. Left unmanaged, it can contribute to shorter adult height, psychosocial distress, and other complications. Two forms exist:

  • Central precocious puberty is the most common. In most girls, no underlying medical cause has been discovered. Treatment often involves puberty blockers.
  • Peripheral precocious puberty may result from tumors, genetic conditions, or exposure to hormone creams.

[Get This Free Download: Learn How Hormones Impact ADHD Symptoms]

Early Puberty and ADHD

When estrogen and progesterone rise in girls, ADHD symptoms fluctuate. Here’s how:

The Emotional Impact

Rising hormone levels affect the physiological and neural systems involved in emotional processing and regulation.

  • More rumination: Girls who experience early puberty are more likely to dwell on negative thoughts and worries, a pattern associated with anxiety and depression.
  • Less equipped to regulate: Hormonal changes also affect the autonomic nervous system, which helps the body regulate emotions. Resting respiratory sinus arrhythmia (RSA) — a variation in heart rate linked to breathing — is an indicator of physiological readiness for emotion regulation that tends to be lower in girls with ADHD.

Acute ADHD Symptom Fluctuation

Girls who menstruate may also experience pronounced changes in ADHD symptoms across the menstrual cycle.

  • The same girl may appear highly symptomatic at one point in the month and minimally symptomatic at another.
  • Low estrogen phases, including the beginning of the menstrual cycle, may intensify inattention and hyperactivity.

Psychological and Social Impacts

Girls who mature early may feel distressed when their physical development outpaces their cognitive, emotional, and social development.

  • A mature appearance may expose them to attention and expectations they are not prepared to manage.
  • Visible physical differences from peers may increase self-consciousness and preoccupation with weight. Some girls attempt to control their bodies through purging or other disordered eating behaviors.

[Read: The Menstrual Cycle Impacts ADHD Symptoms in Disparate Ways]

How to Protect and Empower Young Girls

  • Treat their ADHD. Accurate ADHD assessment and treatment is critical for protecting girls’ mental health. To ensure this, clinicians must understand the distinct and often subtle ways symptoms can appear during adolescence. Hyperactivity, for example, may present as excessive talking or heightened sociability.
  • Impart knowledge. Girls who are equipped with an understanding about puberty feel much more confident about managing changes. When needed, enlist an older family member or another trusted adult with whom your child feels comfortable talking.
  • Seek support. Remind your child that puberty looks different for everyone. If they need help with emotional regulation, find a mental health clinician who is well-versed in the Unified Protocol, a treatment that teaches emotional regulation skills across diagnoses.

How to deal with anxious-ambivalent attachment in a relationship

📅 Oct 22, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

Wondering if anxious-ambivalent attachment is affecting your relationships? Learn the signs, causes, and 10 tips to support yourself or a partner with this attachment style.

Have you ever texted your partner only to spiral into oblivion waiting for them to respond? You wonder if they’re upset with you, if you did something wrong, or if they’re quietly pulling away. When their response finally comes through, you exhale the biggest sigh of relief. And then you start second-guessing why you were so worried in the first place. 

If this sounds familiar, you may be dabbling in the world of ambivalent attachment, which is one of many attachment styles formed in adolescence and carried into adulthood.

Ambivalent attachment is a relationship pattern where you want to feel close to your partner but can’t always get there. It can show up as craving reassurance but doubting it, swinging between feeling connected to your partner and fearing they’ll leave, or analyzing small interactions for a hidden meaning that often isn’t there. While it may feel overwhelming, this attachment style isn’t a flaw in your personality, but rather a pattern shaped by early experiences in your life.

Let’s unpack what ambivalent attachment means, how to spot the signs in adulthood, and what to do if you (or your partner) lean toward this attachment style. Plus, we’ll dive into how to create steadier, more supportive relationships that feel good for all involved.

 

What is an anxious-ambivalent attachment style?

Anxious-ambivalent attachment—also called ambivalent attachment—is a relationship pattern where you crave reassurance yet struggle to feel settled once it’s given. It’s like wanting to lean on someone but worrying they might step away just as you do.

In adult relationships, ambivalent attachment may show up as feeling unsure about your partner’s commitment to you, cycling between connection and doubt, or getting caught in emotional highs and lows depending on how available your partner feels to you. 

While it can feel overwhelming, this isn’t a flaw. It’s a protective strategy learned early in life that can be reshaped over time.

 

What causes an ambivalent attachment style?

Ambivalent attachment often develops in childhood when caregiving is inconsistent. Basically, sometimes you were nurtured, and sometimes you weren’t. This unpredictability creates uncertainty in children, as they learn that comfort may arrive (but not reliably), so they cling tightly when it does appear. Over time, this wires the nervous system to stay on high alert for signs of loss or disconnection.

While it may seem easy to blame the caregivers involved, that’s not always the best answer or solution. Many people provide inconsistent care because of stress, lack of support, or their own attachment struggles. The good news is that even if this style formed early, it can shift in adulthood through consistent experiences of safety and connection.

 

6 signs of an anxious-ambivalent attachment style in adulthood

Ambivalent attachment doesn’t always look dramatic from the outside. It often shows up in subtle, everyday ways that leave both partners feeling drained or confused. While everyone feels insecure at times, people with an ambivalent attachment style experience these patterns more intensely and more often.

1. Constant need for reassurance: You might ask for confirmation of your partner’s love or commitment regularly through words, texts, or gestures. The tricky part for people with an ambivalent attachment style is that reassurance often doesn’t stick. Even after hearing “I love you,” doubt can creep back quickly, fueling the cycle for reassurance again.

2. Fear of abandonment: Small shifts in a partner’s behavior—like if they take longer than usual to reply to a message or are quieter than usual—can trigger unnecessary worry. The fear isn’t just about being left by your partner, it’s about being forgotten or no longer valued. And for someone with this attachment style, those fears can be dire.

3. Hyper-attunement to signals: People with ambivalent attachment often read between the lines of every interaction, scanning tone, body language, or text punctuation for hidden meaning. A short text that simply says, “K,” might feel like rejection rather than just brevity.

4. Emotional highs and lows: Relationships can feel like a rollercoaster. You may feel ecstatic when things feel close and connected, then anxious when any distance appears. The intensity of these swings can feel overwhelming for both partners.

5. Difficulty trusting stability: Even when the relationship is going well, there’s a lingering sense that it could change at any moment. This makes it hard to relax into intimacy, because safety doesn’t feel fully reliable.

6. Feeling “too much” or “not enough”: Self-doubt often runs in the background, accompanied by persistent worrying that your needs are excessive or fearing that you’re somehow lacking. This inner tug-of-war can make it difficult to feel secure in how you show up in a relationship.

 

How to deal with anxious-ambivalent attachment in yourself or a partner

Working with ambivalent attachment is about finding steadier ground, both within yourself and your relationships. These strategies combine self-regulation, clearer communication, and practical agreements that build trust over time.

If you relate to ambivalent attachment

1. Recognize and name the pattern: The first step is simply noticing when old fears are coming up. Take time to put a name to the feeling, and remind yourself that it’s a learned response and not proof that something is wrong with you or your relationship. Taking a beat to give your feelings a name gives you the chance to pause before you react impulsively.

2. Soothe your body before your thoughts: Ambivalent attachment often activates the nervous system, which can make worries feel louder than they are. Simple grounding tools, such as intentional breathing, a mindful walk, or even running cold water over your wrists (which may help calm your nervous system) can bring your body back to baseline. Once you’re calmer, you’ll have more perspective on what’s really happening.

3. Ask directly instead of protesting: Protest behaviors (testing someone, withdrawing your affection, or sending a string of texts from the top of your mind) can backfire and push your partner away instead of bringing them closer. Practice naming your needs clearly instead. Try saying something like, “I’m feeling anxious right now and could use reassurance that we’re okay.” It might feel vulnerable to be so honest and open, but being direct usually leads to better connection.

4. Build a personal reassurance toolkit: Instead of depending only on your partner for reassurance, create a set of resources you can turn to yourself. This might include rereading kind messages, journaling, doing grounding exercises, or calling a supportive friend. Having options beyond your partner reduces the sense of desperation when anxiety spikes.

5. Practice small steps toward trust: Shifting attachment styles takes time, but small experiments help. Try waiting a little longer than you typically would before sending a check-in text, and see how that feels. You can also remind yourself of past experiences where closeness stood the test of time or survived a disagreement. Each successful moment builds evidence that security is possible, even if your old fears insist otherwise.

💙 Looking for tips to self-soothe? Try the Calming Anxiety guided practice on the Calm app.

 

If your partner relates to ambivalent attachment

6. Offer steady reassurance with boundaries: Consistency may help calm anxiety. Simple habits like a morning check-in or saying when you’ll call back create predictability. But it’s also important to pair reassurance with realistic boundaries. You might say, “I can’t text during work, but I’ll check in at lunch.”

7. Respond to the need, not just the words: Underneath protests or big reactions is usually a fear of being abandoned. Instead of focusing only on the tone or delivery of something your partner says, try reflecting the underlying need. You can say, “I hear that you’re worried I’m pulling away, but I want you to know I’m here.” This validation often matters more than a perfect solution.

8. Set clear repair practices: When tensions rise, agree in advance on how to pause and return to the conversation when you’re level-headed. Try saying, “If one of us feels overwhelmed, we’ll take a 20-minute break and come back at an agreed time.” Returning as promised helps rebuild trust, strengthens your relationship, and makes conflicts feel less threatening.

💙 Need a little more support? Check out the Kind Communication meditation on the Calm app.

Practices for both of you together

9. Create shared routines for connection: Structure helps balance anxiety. A weekly check-in, an agreed-upon text during the workday, or even a daily hug ritual can anchor the relationship. These small but predictable moments reduce the need for constant reassurance.

Related read: How to use words of encouragement: 50 examples

10. Name the cycle and work as a team: Instead of blaming each other, talk about the pattern: “We tend to argue when I get quiet, and you seek more closeness. How can we catch it earlier?” Naming the cycle makes it something you face together, rather than a flaw in either partner.

💙 Learn how to take A Purposeful Pause with Jay Shetty on the Calm app.

 

Ambivalent attachment FAQs

What causes ambivalent attachment?

Ambivalent attachment usually begins in early childhood. When a caregiver is sometimes warm and attentive but other times distracted, stressed, or unavailable, a child learns that love is unpredictable. This inconsistency creates anxiety about whether comfort will be there when it’s needed most. 

Over time, the child develops strategies to cling tightly to connection, like seeking closeness while bracing for it to disappear. It’s not always about neglect. Often, caregivers themselves are overwhelmed or carrying their own attachment wounds. But the result is the same, and it’s that love is both essential and unreliable.

How do I know if I have ambivalent attachment?

You may notice repeating patterns in your relationships, like craving reassurance but rarely feeling settled when you receive it. Small shifts in your partner’s tone, attention, or texting habits may trigger outsized worry about abandonment. 

Overthinking, analyzing, or swinging between feeling deeply connected and then uncertain are also common. If these patterns show up across multiple relationships, that can be a strong sign of ambivalent attachment.

Can an ambivalent attachment style change over time?

Attachment styles can change. While your early experiences lay the foundation, your attachment style can shift as you build new experiences of safety and stability. Supportive relationships, therapy, and personal work on self-regulation all help strengthen secure attachment. 

The process is gradual and often happens through repeated moments of repair and reassurance. Over time, these experiences retrain your nervous system to trust that closeness can last.

What’s the difference between ambivalent and avoidant attachment?

Both ambivalent and avoidant attachment come from early inconsistencies in caregiving, but they lead to opposite coping strategies. People with ambivalent attachment often lean in, seeking closeness, reassurance, and connection. Sometimes, these manifest in ways that feel intense or overwhelming to their partner. 

People with avoidant attachment tend to lean out. They value independence, minimize their needs, and withdraw when relationships feel too vulnerable. Ambivalent attachment is marked by fear of abandonment, while avoidant attachment is marked by discomfort with intimacy.

Can therapy help with ambivalent attachment?

Yes, therapy can be effective. Working with an attachment-focused therapist gives you a consistent relationship where trust and stability are practiced week after week. Different forms of therapy like Emotionally Focused Therapy (EFT), schema therapy, and cognitive-behavioral therapy can help you identify old patterns, calm the nervous system, and create new relational habits. 

Over time, the therapy relationship itself often serves as a secure base, making it easier to bring those new ways of relating into your everyday life.

How can I deal with an ambivalently attached partner?

Patience and predictability are key. Your partner may need more reassurance than you thought, so offering it consistently and directly can ease their anxiety. For example, sending a quick “thinking of you” text before a long meeting or confirming plans in advance can make a big difference. 

At the same time, it’s important to set boundaries around what you can realistically give. A helpful approach is to offer what you can with warmth, and state what you can’t with clarity. That way, your partner feels cared for without you becoming overextended.

Calm your mind. Change your life.

Mental health is hard. Getting support doesn't have to be. The Calm app puts the tools to feel better in your back pocket, with personalized content to manage stress and anxiety, get better sleep, and feel more present in your life.

A surprising two-year clinical trial found that fish oil supplements successfully delivered omega-3s to the brain but failed to improve memory or protect against Alzheimer's-related brain shrinkage. Researchers suspect that getting these nutrients through a healthy diet may offer benefits that supplements alone cannot provide.

What is a circadian rhythm disorder? Plus, 6 ways to treat it

📅 Oct 21, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Clinically reviewed by Dr. Chris Mosunic, PhD, RD, MBA

A circadian rhythm sleep disorder can leave you tired at all the wrong times. Learn the symptoms, what disrupts your rhythm, and 6 ways to treat a CRSD.

You’re tired when you should be awake, or wired when you should be asleep. Whether it’s 2am and you’re staring at the ceiling, your body still hasn’t recovered from that red-eye flight, or the night shift is taking its toll, the struggle is the same: your internal clock and your life are pulling in opposite directions. 

These are circadian rhythm sleep disorders (CRSDs), and they happen when your body's sleep-wake cycle doesn't match your daily schedule. And they're more common than you'd think — often mistaken for stress or brushed off as one more thing to deal with once life slows down.

The good news is that CRSDs are medically recognized and treatable. Below, we’ll cover the different types, why they happen, and how to get your sleep back on track.

 

What is a circadian rhythm sleep disorder?

Your body has an internal clock—the circadian rhythm—that regulates when you feel sleepy and when you feel alert. It runs on roughly a 24-hour cycle, shaped by external cues like light, temperature, and meal timing.

A circadian rhythm sleep disorder (CRSD) occurs when your internal clock consistently falls out of sync with your environment, social schedule, or desired sleep times. 

There are several distinct types of circadian rhythm sleep disorders:

  • Delayed sleep phase disorder (DSPD): Sleep comes much later than usual (often in the early hours of the morning) followed by a struggle to wake up on time.

  • Advanced sleep phase disorder (ASPD): Sleep arrives early in the evening, and waking happens in the early hours of the morning — the reverse of DSPD.

  • Non-24-hour sleep-wake disorder: Sleep and wake times drift later or earlier each day instead of settling into a 24-hour rhythm. This pattern is most common in people who are blind.

  • Irregular sleep-wake rhythm disorder: Sleep is broken into several short stretches across the day and night, without a clear pattern.

  • Shift work disorder: Night or rotating shift schedules conflict with the body's natural sleep timing.

  • Jet lag disorder: A temporary misalignment between the body’s internal clock and local time after crossing time zones.

What are the symptoms of a circadian rhythm sleep disorder?

Symptoms vary based on the type of CRSD, but the core issue is the same: sleep happens at the wrong time. 

You might experience: 

  • trouble falling asleep at a normal bedtime

  • difficulty waking up in the morning, or waking too early

  • poor concentration or memory

  • daytime sleepiness 

  • interrupted sleep patterns 

  • mood changes, including irritability, anxiety, or low mood

  • reduced performance at work or school

 

Why do circadian rhythm sleep disorders happen?

Circadian rhythm sleep disorders often stem from multiple factors at once, ranging from lifestyle and environment to biology.

Light exposure: Light entering your eyes is the strongest external cue for your internal clock to stay in sync with the outside world. Too much bright light at night, like from screens, or too little natural light in the morning can push your clock in the wrong direction.

Genetics: Your genes can set your natural sleep timing earlier or later than average. Certain gene variants have been linked to differences in circadian timing, which may explain a lifelong tendency toward being a night owl or an early riser.

Age: Your sleep timing usually changes with age. Teenagers drift toward later sleep and wake times, while older adults often feel tired earlier in the evening and wake earlier as a result. 

Shift work and irregular schedules: Night or rotating shifts keep you awake when your body expects to be asleep. Irregular eating and exercise can add to that disruption.

Neurological conditions: Conditions such as Alzheimer's disease, Parkinson's disease, or a history of traumatic brain injury are tied to a higher risk of circadian disruption, including fragmented or irregular sleep. Researchers are still working out whether disrupted rhythms are a cause, an early sign, or both.

Blindness: Without light entering the eyes, the brain's clock loses its main external cue for staying aligned with a 24-hour day. 

How to treat circadian rhythm sleep disorders: 6 tips for better sleep

No matter which type of CRSD you're dealing with, you can get your internal clock back on track. Here's what works, and how to find the right approach for you.

1. Use light to reset your internal clock

In the morning: Bright daylight early in the day helps advance your clock, which is especially useful for delayed sleep phase disorder. During darker months, using a bright light therapy box (10,000 lux) for 20–30 minutes after waking can substitute for sunlight. If you have any eye conditions, it's a good idea to check with a doctor before starting.

In the evening: Reduce bright light 2–3 hours before bed to ease your body into sleep mode. Screens and overhead lighting can suppress melatonin production, while dimmer, warmer light helps your body settle down. 

Traveling across time zones? The strategy for beating jet lag with light depends on which direction you're flying. Seek bright light in the new morning and avoid it in the new evening when flying east. Do the reverse when flying west. A day or two of light timing can speed up your adjustment.

💙 Pair your morning light with a Morning Meditation from the Daily Calm. 

2. Keep consistent sleep and wake times

One of the most effective ways to steady your circadian rhythm is to maintain a regular sleep schedule. Wake up at the same time every day—even on weekends—to give your body a consistent cue to work with. It may feel hard at first, but with consistency, your body naturally adjusts to the rhythm.

Working nights or rotating shifts? "Consistent" doesn't have to mean conventional. Try anchoring your sleep to the same block of hours on workdays, and limiting how far you drift on days off.

💙 Build a sleep routine with Making Progress from the 7 Days of Sleep series on Calm. 

 

3. Reset your schedule gradually with chronotherapy

Chronotherapy involves slowly adjusting your sleep and wake times in one direction, a little each day, until you reach your target schedule. 

Dealing with delayed sleep phase disorder? This might mean going to bed a few hours later each day (moving all the way around the clock) until you land on your desired bedtime. It's a demanding process, and there's some risk of your schedule drifting further off track, so it's best done under a sleep specialist's guidance rather than on your own.

A sleep specialist can help tailor chronotherapy to your specific pattern.

4. Wind down with a mindfulness practice

Sleep difficulties often come with anxiety about sleep itself, which can make it harder to fall asleep. Mindfulness practices can help calm your racing mind and relieve that anxiety. Even a few minutes of mindful breathing or a short guided meditation can quiet your mind enough to help you drift off.

💙 Try this Wind Down (for Sleep) meditation on Calm.

5. Time your melatonin carefully 

Melatonin is a hormone your brain produces, and its levels naturally rise in the evening — signaling to your body that sleep time is approaching. Think of a melatonin supplement as a timing tool rather than a sedative that will help you fall asleep on the spot. 

Dealing with delayed sleep phase disorder? Take a low dose (around 0.5–3 mg) three hours before your desired bedtime to help adjust your clock gradually. A doctor or sleep specialist can advise on the right amount for your situation.

Traveling across time zones? Melatonin taken around your target bedtime (about an hour before) in the new time zone is a well-supported way to adjust to jet lag faster.

💙 Discover the best time to take melatonin for restful sleep. 

6. Talk to a doctor or sleep specialist

For persistent or severe circadian rhythm sleep disorders, professional support can make a real difference. A sleep specialist may recommend a formal evaluation, actigraphy (a wrist sensor that tracks sleep-wake patterns over time), or a combination of treatments. 

Struggling with non-24-hour or irregular sleep-wake rhythm disorder? These patterns respond less predictably to light and schedule changes on their own. A sleep specialist can build a plan around your specific pattern.

Cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia (CBT-I) is also sometimes paired with circadian-focused treatments. It’s a structured therapy that targets the thoughts and habits keeping you awake. 

 

Circadian rhythm disorder FAQs

What disrupts our circadian rhythm?

Several everyday habits can throw off your circadian rhythm, including irregular sleep schedules, shift work, jet lag, too much screen use at night, and inconsistent light exposure. Stress, alcohol, caffeine, and some medications can also throw off your body's timing. 

Can a circadian rhythm disorder go away on its own?

Some circadian rhythm sleep disorders can go away on their own, but it depends on the type you're dealing with. Jet lag usually clears up within a few days as your body adjusts to a new time zone. But most chronic circadian rhythm sleep disorders don't resolve without changes to your sleep schedule, light exposure, or daily habits. The sooner you start addressing it, the more manageable the shift becomes. 

Is circadian rhythm disorder the same as insomnia?

Not quite, though circadian rhythm sleep disorder and insomnia can feel similar. Insomnia is trouble falling or staying asleep, no matter the time of day. A circadian rhythm sleep disorder is sleep happening at the wrong time. Someone with delayed sleep phase disorder, for example, might sleep just fine from 3am to 11am, instead of a more typical schedule. 

How long does it take to reset my circadian rhythm?

The time it takes to reset your circadian rhythm depends on how far your body clock has shifted. Jet lag typically settles within a few days, but a significantly delayed or advanced sleep schedule can take several weeks of consistent habit changes to correct. Light therapy and melatonin can speed the process along, but there's no quick fix. 

Does melatonin help circadian rhythm sleep disorders?

Melatonin can help with circadian rhythm sleep disorders, but the timing matters. For delayed sleep phase disorder, a low dose taken several hours before your target bedtime can help shift your clock gradually. For jet lag, taking it closer to your target bedtime tends to work better. A doctor or sleep specialist can help you figure out the right dose and timing for your situation.

When should I see a doctor about my sleep schedule?

If your sleep schedule has been getting in the way of work, relationships, or daily life—and adjusting your habits hasn’t helped—it's worth reaching out to a doctor or sleep specialist. They can assess whether you have a circadian rhythm sleep disorder and recommend a treatment plan. 

💙 Learn more about the 7 most common sleep disorders. 

Can I fix my circadian rhythm naturally?

In many cases, you can fix your circadian rhythm naturally. Focus on getting bright light in the morning and keeping consistent sleep and wake times. For milder patterns, these changes alone can make a noticeable difference, though more serious cases may need extra support.

Calm your mind. Change your life.

Mental health is hard. Getting support doesn't have to be. The Calm app puts the tools to feel better in your back pocket, with personalized content to manage stress and anxiety, get better sleep, and feel more present in your life.

Flu season is early this year. Here’s how to actually protect your family, according to experts

📅 Oct 16, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It’s almost that time of year when a small cornucopia of medicine takes up residence on the kitchen counter and the house becomes a chorus of coughs and sneezes. As a parent, you know the particular dread of seeing the school nurse’s number flashing on your phone, because you know it’s only a matter of hours until the whole family is flat out.

Unfortunately that call may be right around the corner: Flu season is also arriving months earlier than usual this year. 

Experts say it’s a good idea to prepare now before the first fever strikes. Most of what actually protects a family is simple, though, and you don’t need a cabinet full of products or a nursing degree to do it well.

For real advice, we talked to two physicians who’ve spent this fall fielding questions from worried parents. Tracy Tyson, MD and Brittany Odom, MD, are both board-certified pediatricians and moms themselves. Here’s what they want you to know about how to protect your family from the flu this season.

Related Is it the flu, RSV, a cold, allergies or Covid?

Is flu season really starting early this year?

Yes, in parts of the country. “We have started seeing influenza earlier this year, and nationwide reports are also showing an earlier flu season,” Dr. Tyson says. The picture is uneven, though. “Nationally, the latest CDC report shows flu activity is still relatively low but increasing, with the highest activity on the West Coast,” Dr. Odom says, and she’s already seeing plenty of cases in her area. Regions surge at slightly different times, she says, reason enough to get ahead of it now. “You don’t need to wait for flu to fill your child’s classroom before getting them protected.”

When should kids get the flu shot, and is it too late?

Get it as soon as it’s available, both doctors say. “I recommend getting it in September or October, ideally by Halloween, for everyone 6 months and older,” Dr. Odom says. November isn’t too late, she adds, but she wouldn’t wait on purpose, since protection takes about 2 weeks to build and some kids ages 6 months through 8 years need 2 doses at least 4 weeks apart. 

“It isn’t ever too late, but the earlier, the better,” Dr. Tyson says. The flu and COVID shots can be given at the same visit, with no required spacing. “As a mom, I know how quickly something on the calendar can get pushed back,” Dr. Odom adds. “So if you have the opportunity now, take it.”

Is the nasal spray or the flu shot better for kids?

Either one works, if your child is eligible, both doctors say. The shot is approved for children 6 months and up. The nasal spray, or flu mist, is an option from age 2, with limits. “Children ages 2 through 4 with asthma or wheezing in the past year shouldn’t receive the nasal spray, and older children with asthma or other medical conditions need an eligibility review,” Dr. Odom says. Dr. Tyson draws the same line for the mist. Past that, it comes down to your kid. “My priority is an appropriate vaccine that your child actually receives,” Dr. Odom says.

Does the flu shot actually work if my kid gets sick anyway?

The shot lowers the odds of catching the flu and softens its severity when it breaks through. Dr. Tyson likes an old health-department line. “Taking flu from wild to mild is exactly the right way of thinking,” she says, estimating it prevents flu in up to 60% of cases. It targets influenza specifically, not the parade of other bugs your kid hauls home from daycare. “A sick day after vaccination doesn’t mean the vaccine had no value,” Dr. Odom says, since even a vaccinated child who gets the flu is less likely to end up seriously ill.

How do you keep the flu from spreading through the house?

If one kid goes down, the rest of the house doesn’t necessarily have to fall like dominos. You absolutely can limit the spread, Dr. Tyson says. The moves that help are unglamorous. Wash hands, stop sharing cups and utensils, cough into an elbow, and separate sleeping spaces where you can. Vaccination cuts household spread too.

You don’t need to disinfect every surface. “Don’t put all your energy into wiping down the house while overlooking the air everyone is sharing,” Dr. Odom says. Crack a window, run a HEPA air purifier, or run the HVAC fan while someone is sick. Masks help during close contact, though never on a child under 2. 

And it’s not a failure if another family member gets sick.. “Viruses are sneaky, it happens,” Dr. Odom says.

What flu symptoms in kids mean it’s time to call the doctor?

 Most healthy, older kids can ride out the flu at home, but call your doctor if:

  • Your child is under 5  or has a condition like asthma or a weak immune system. Call early, Dr. Odom says, since antiviral treatment works best when started fast.
  • A fever lasts more than 3 days in an older child, or more than 24 hours in a child under 2. 
  • Symptoms get better and then come back, or your child is getting worse or barely drinking.
  • Their breathing, hydration or energy worries you. 
  • Trouble breathing, ribs pulling in, blue lips, difficulty waking, a seizure, no urine for 8 or more hours, or a fever of 100.4°F or higher in a baby under 3 months require urgent medical attention, not just a call to your pediatrician. 
Related The unexpected flu symptom every parent should be aware of

How do you treat the flu at home, and how high is too high for a fever?

Keep a few basics on hand, Dr. Odom says: a thermometer, an oral rehydration solution like Pedialyte, saline drops or spray, and an age-appropriate fever medicine with a dosing syringe. An at-home combination test (which generally covers flu A and B, COVID and RSV in one swab) can also help you tell the flu from the other bugs going around. Care itself is mostly rest, small frequent sips of fluid and saline for congestion. For babies, keep offering breast milk or formula. Honey can soothe a cough for kids 1 and older, but never for a baby under 1.

For fever and aches, acetaminophen helps, and ibuprofen is fine from 6 months, though skip it if your child is dehydrated or vomiting a lot, Dr. Odom says. Dose by weight and use the syringe, not a kitchen spoon, she adds, and never give aspirin to a child with flu.

On how high is too high, the number matters less than you’d think. “There isn’t a level of fever that is unsafe per se,” Dr. Tyson says. “It’s more about the length of time and the symptoms with the fever.” Call the doctor for a baby under 3 months at 100.4°F or higher, and for an older child whose fever repeatedly tops 104°F or won’t come down with medicine. The goal, Dr. Odom says, is a child comfortable enough to drink and rest.

How do I protect a newborn or pregnant family member during flu season?

The most vulnerable people in a house can’t protect themselves, so the job falls on everyone else. Babies under 6 months are too young for the flu vaccine, which makes vaccinating the adults and older kids at home especially important, Dr. Odom says. Keep sick visitors away, improve airflow, and for newborns, the advice is obvious but still worth stating since the desire is strong: “No kissing the babies!” The flu shot is safe in any trimester of pregnancy and protects the baby after birth too. For anyone pregnant, immunocompromised or caring for a newborn, don’t wait out a symptom or a known exposure. Call your provider promptly about treatment or prevention.

Related Which cold and flu medications can I take while breastfeeding?

When should I reach out if I’m worried?

Asked what they most wish parents knew, both doctors have the same advice: reach out sooner. “You don’t have to wait until your child looks terribly sick to call us,” Dr. Odom says. “Flu has treatments, and timing matters. Asking for help early is part of taking good care of your child, not overreacting.” 

6 ways to make mornings smoother when your child has ADHD

📅 Oct 15, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It’s 7:40, the bus comes at 7:52, and one shoe is missing. You’ve asked about the backpack four times. Your kid is half-dressed on the floor, watching dust move through a sunbeam.

If that’s your morning, you have company. About 1 in 9 US children, roughly 7.1 million kids, has been diagnosed with ADHD, according to CDC data. For a lot of those families, the hardest stretch of the day happens before anyone heads out the door.

There’s a reason mornings hit this hard, and it isn’t that your kid doesn’t care. Michael Morse, MD, a board-certified child and adolescent psychiatrist who directs the ADHD & Anxiety Center at Alvord Baker and is an associate professor of psychiatry at Georgetown University, says the morning routine requires a child “to do the most with the least help.” Many kids with ADHD run on a later internal clock, he says, so at 7 a.m. the brain regions that handle planning and time aren’t fully up and running, and yet, that’s when we ask kids to move through a string of boring steps against a deadline.

Executive function, the mental system for getting started, tracking time and shifting gears, tends to develop more slowly in kids with ADHD, so what looks like refusal is often a brain that can’t get itself going yet without help. “Kids do well when they can,” Morse says. For parents, that means making a mental shift from asking why your kid won’t do something to examining what’s getting in the way. That reframe can often help you resist a lecture and lend a hand instead. “Punishment can’t teach a skill a child doesn’t have yet,” he says, “but support can.”

Related 6 things I’ve learned about supporting my daughter with ADHD and SPD

Here are six ADHD morning routine strategies Morse says actually help make it smoother for everyone.

1. Build the morning the night before

“The best mornings usually start the evening before, when there’s time to think and nobody is racing the bus,” Morse says. Sign the permission slips, settle on what’s for breakfast, and, if your kid is old enough, have them pack their backpack and lay out clothes for the next day. (For younger kids, you’ll want to supervise this process.)

He also suggests a five-minute planning talk earlier in the day to look at what’s ahead for the day together, whether that’s gym, a test or a game after school, which helps a kid build their own sense of what’s coming.

Sleep is the biggest lever of all. “A tired brain makes every ADHD symptom worse,” Morse says, and it gets less benefit from medication. Sleep trouble is common here, affecting as many as half of kids with ADHD by some estimates. A steady bedtime, screens off about an hour before bed, and a warm bath or shower all make it easier for your child to nod off at night, which sets the stage for smoother mornings.

2. Put the steps where your kid can see them

Kids with ADHD often can’t hold a string of instructions in mind, so “‘go get ready’ is really six instructions in three words,” Morse says. A visual sequence takes that load off their memory. In practice it’s a simple chart on the bathroom mirror listing each step in order: get dressed, eat breakfast, brush teeth, shoes on, backpack. Use pictures for younger kids and let them check off each step as they go, and build the chart together, since kids are more likely to follow a plan they helped make.

When your kid stalls, skip the fresh reminder and point back to the list with a quick “What’s next on your chart?” Do it long enough and “the chart becomes the reminder instead of the parent,” Morse says.

Related Paris Hilton’s morning fix for ADHD moms: This night-before routine

3. Make time something they can see

Many kids with ADHD experience time differently, something “called ‘time blindness,’ though some people prefer not to use that term,” Morse says. Either way, 10 minutes from now and an hour from now can feel about the same, so the tools that help are the ones that make time visible. A visual timer with a colored disk that shrinks as the minutes run out lets a kid see at a glance how much is left, and timers that announce the minutes work well for a kid dressing in another room.

Morse says “a simple timer that does one thing beats a phone,” since phones are designed to pull attention toward games and messages, the opposite of what a kid needs while getting ready.

4. Cut the number of decisions

Every choice in the morning is another chance to stall, and each stall may need an adult to jump-start things again. So take the decisions out ahead of time. Have your child select their outfit the night before or keep a few go-to options on rotation. “Some kids wear something close to a uniform and are relieved not to have to think about it,” Morse says. For breakfast, offer two pre-approved options, ideally with some protein like eggs or yogurt to keep energy steady throughout the day. 

Give the backpack, shoes and jacket a single launch spot, one basket or hook where they always live, so nobody’s hunting a missing shoe at 7:40. The thread running through all of it, in Morse’s words, is “moving the thinking out of the morning and into the calm of the evening. Fewer decisions also mean fewer arguments.”

5. Ask your prescriber about medication timing

If your kid takes medication, when they take it can matter as much as whether they take it. “No ADHD medicine works the moment it’s swallowed,” Morse says, so a long-acting stimulant taken at breakfast may not kick in until a child is already at school, leaving the hardest part of the morning uncovered. Some families, with their doctor’s guidance, give the dose closer to wake-up so it has more time to start working, and some formulations are made to be taken the night before.

This one is a conversation for your prescriber rather than a change to make on your own. Timing is one factor that can impact a medication’s efficacy, so it’s worth raising at the next appointment.

6. When it falls apart, choose the relationship over the lecture

“A few fixes come from love and still backfire,” Morse says. Dressing a dawdling second grader or racing a forgotten worksheet to school smooths the morning but teaches a kid they can’t manage on their own. Repeatedly raising your voice tends to pull everyone into a louder and louder standoff. And consequences rarely do the teaching, because “the problem was memory, not caring,” Morse says.

When a morning does come apart, the goal is to keep it from becoming a bad day between you and your kid. Once everyone’s out the door, the moment for teaching has passed, and a lecture in the car just sends a child into school ashamed and anxious. Try something simple instead, like “That was a rough morning. I love you, and we’ll figure it out.” 

Related How to get your kids to listen—without yelling

Later, when everyone’s calm, look at what broke together, since it’s usually one step you can fix without overhauling the whole routine. Go easy on yourself too. (And if you need a bit of support, Morse points parents to the work of psychologist Kristin Neff on self-compassion.)

None of this makes mornings frictionless. It hands the remembering to a system instead of to you, so you’re not the last thing standing between your kid and the 7:52 bus.

Health sharing plans have moved from a faith-based niche into a mainstream budgeting conversation. But before you switch, it’s worth understanding exactly what you’re trading away, not just what you’re saving. What Is...
